The argument it rests on is one line of robust statistics: the arithmetic mean has a **breakdown point of zero** (Rousseeuw & Leroy §1.2 [rousseeuw1987]), so a single unit reporting an arbitrary value moves the combined rate by an arbitrary amount — and it does so *while that unit's own validity flag still reads true*, because a gyro that has failed high has no way to know. Averaging therefore converts a survivable single-unit fault into a vehicle-level one, which is the exact inverse of what the third IMU is carried for (Gilmore & McKern's redundancy-management treatment for strapdown inertial arrays, [gilmore1972]). In order: **per-unit plausibility gates** (finiteness, then rate magnitude against a configured *vehicle* limit — not the gyro's measurement range, which is what a railed unit reports); then **per-axis median** over the survivors at three or more units, whose breakdown point is ⌊(n−1)/2⌋/n; then exclusion as an FDIR event. The library is generic over N and implements the whole ladder, but **which rung the vehicle flies is a configuration decision**, and the reference vehicle carries **two** IMUs rather than three (user decision, 2026-08-02) — mass and cost against a third unit. So the flown branch is the pairwise one below, and the median branch is the one a heavier vehicle inherits already tested. Weighted fusion of the survivors is deliberately not done: on a homogeneous redundant set equal weights already *are* the optimal weights, and a weighting a marginal unit could skew would give back the property the module exists for. Parity space [gilmore1972] is the sharper instrument — with four or more non-coplanar sensitive axes it *isolates* rather than out-votes, and detects far below the plausibility limit — and is named as the upgrade path for a skewed or dissimilar array, because it needs the array geometry, which this vehicle's configuration does not carry.

**The two-unit branch, which is the one that flies.** A pair can *detect* a disagreement and never *attribute* one — it says only that one of the two is lying — so identification needs a third information source, and the one the vehicle has is the MEKF's propagated body rate: the unit the filter's own dynamics disbelieve loses, is latched out with reason `OUTVOTED`, and falls under the same re-admission policy as a gate failure, so FDIR has one path to reason about however the fault was found. The reference used is the previous cycle's published bias-corrected rate, which is genuinely independent of the faulted reading because the vote runs before the update that would fold it in.

**Three guards make that identification safe, and each closes a way it would otherwise be wrong.** *The reference is range-checked*, against the same physical rate limit the measurements are held to and not merely for finiteness: it comes from the estimator's own published state, so an upstream fault can hand the vote an absurd value, and an unchecked one makes *both* residuals enormous and awards the identification to whichever unit happens to sit nearer to nonsense — latching out the healthy unit. A failing reference is treated as no reference, which is the honest ambiguous outcome. *The comparison must be decisive*, not merely ordered: the loser outside the disagreement gate while the winner is inside it, so the reference genuinely agrees with one reading and disagrees with the other. Ordering alone is a coin flip exactly where it matters — a slow common-mode drift, where both units are wrong by similar amounts — and it also disposes of the exact tie for free, since equal residuals can never satisfy both halves. *And the verdict must repeat*, over `ImuIdentifyConfirmCycles` consecutive cycles, because a latch is permanent until re-admission earns it back and one sample must not buy one; a verdict that flips between units resets both counts and never confirms, which is the correct outcome for a disagreement the reference cannot resolve. The winner is published *during* confirmation rather than withheld, and that is load-bearing rather than lenient: the reference is the estimator's own published rate, so withholding it would starve the next cycle of the very reference this branch needs and the count could never accumulate. It costs detection latency, not rate availability. A related mitigation is worth naming because it is real but not sufficient: the residual is taken against the *filter's* rate, which is bias-corrected, so a slow drift shared by both units is partly absorbed into the MEKF's own bias state rather than appearing in both residuals — but bias observability depends on the vector measurements the filter is getting, so it cannot be relied on through eclipse or a measurement outage, and the margin and confirmation gates are what make the identification safe without it.

**Re-admission is judged on the criterion that excluded the unit**, and this is the one place the policy could quietly invert itself. A gate failure is undone by passing that gate. An *identified* unit was plausible by construction — it passed every per-unit gate and lost a comparison — so counting plausibility towards its re-admission would return it unconditionally, and it would be outvoted again on the very next cycle: a permanent exclude/re-admit flap at the re-admission period, with an FDIR event on every lap, which is exactly the "one event on the transition" contract the policy exists to keep. An identified unit therefore accrues credit only on cycles where it **agrees with the combination**, which is judged after the combination exists rather than before it.

That leaves the case where **no filter solution is available** — cold start before the MEKF has seeded, or after a demotion. The §9.2 response chosen there is **no rate at all**, which lands the estimator in the dropout behaviour it is already designed for: attitude held, covariance growing, with an edge-gated `ImuVoteAmbiguous` naming the disagreement's size.

Edge-gating is right for a transient and wrong for a permanent fault, so the alert alone would leave a vehicle flying rate-less indefinitely after a single warning — and the coast-horizon escalation that might have caught it does not fire, because a gyro fault leaves the *vector* pairs untouched and TRIAD keeps the attitude alive. The response is therefore **persistence-counted**: after `ImuAmbiguityEscalateCycles` of continuous ambiguity the estimator emits `ImuVoteAmbiguousPersistent` naming the elapsed duration and the cycles spent without a rate, re-emitted at that same period while the condition lasts — bounded, not per cycle. The duration is measured from the clock rather than inferred from a cycle count and an assumed rate, since the rate group is configuration and cycles are also lost to refusals. **The onboard response is refusal plus escalation and nothing more, and that boundary is deliberate:** choosing between two disagreeing units without evidence is precisely what the refusal exists to avoid, so no autonomous action beats continuing to refuse. Recovery is a ground action — disambiguate from telemetry, then `RESET_ESTIMATOR` once the faulty unit is known, or uplink a widened `ImuDisagreementRadps` if the pair is merely out of family. An autonomous FDIR *mode* response is explicitly deferred to the §9 FDIR push, which owns the mode ladder; this event is the signal that push will consume. This is a deliberate non-monotonicity against the single-unit case, and the reason is that one unit is *no evidence* of a fault while two disagreeing units are evidence with no attribution; publishing either would propagate a possibly-railed rate into the attitude, which the coast does not. On a two-IMU vehicle that is a reachable *flight* state rather than an already-degraded one, which is exactly why it is chosen and documented rather than fallen into — it is the price of the second unit instead of the third, and it is the one thing a third IMU would buy that the pair cannot.

**Absence is not implausibility:** a stale or dropped-out unit neither latches an exclusion nor advances the re-admission credit, so a bus dropout does not leave the ground reasoning about a fault that was never reported and an excluded unit cannot serve out its sentence by going quiet.

**Exclusion is an FDIR response with an explicit recovery policy (§9.2).** A unit that fails a gate is latched out and reported once on the transition — `ImuUnitExcluded(unit, NOT_FINITE|RATE_LIMIT|OUTVOTED)`, so a permanently dead unit costs one event rather than ten a second, and the event names the gate because "expect recovery or do not" is the actionable part. Re-admission is **automatic with hysteresis**: `ImuReadmitCycles` consecutive plausible cycles (1 s at the 10 Hz rate on the reference tuning), chosen over command-only because the dominant real cause of one implausible sample is transient — a bus glitch, a dropped frame, a shock past the modelled rate limit — and spending a unit of redundancy permanently on a transient is the more expensive error; the consecutive count is what stops a marginal unit flapping, and a genuinely dead unit never accumulates it. `RESET_ESTIMATOR` is the commanded path and drops every latch at once, on the same reasoning that governs the rest of that command: an operator saying "start over" is different information from a unit having behaved for N cycles. The array's health is one telemetry word (`ImuExclusionMask`) plus `ImuContributing`, which is the redundancy margin read directly — 2 is the healthy reference vehicle (detection, and attribution while fine mode holds), 1 has lost detection as well, 0 means no rate; a vehicle carrying three or more reads 3+ and takes the median branch, which needs no external reference at all. The three tuning values (`ImuMaxRateRadps`, `ImuDisagreementRadps`, `ImuReadmitCycles`) ride in the **coarse** validity gate rather than a set of their own: without a voted rate there is no gyro propagation, so a missing value costs exactly what a missing `SigmaSunWhiteRad` costs, and a separate gate would imply a degraded-but-flying state that does not exist.

**The sun-sensor suite, and the coverage number the accuracy claim rests on.** The vehicle carries six GomSpace FSS units on the six body faces, `ss_zp` (the solar-array normal, the face Sun-Point and Safe put on the Sun) keeping index 0 and the accuracy budget it was measured on. Each 60° acceptance half-angle covers the solid-angle fraction `(1 − cos 60°)/2 = 0.25` of the sphere, and six cones on the face normals cover it **completely**: the worst-placed direction is a body diagonal at `arccos(1/√3) = 54.736°` from each of the three nearest normals. Dropping one face — the ±X/±Y/+Z pattern — leaves 8.8% of the sphere seen by nothing, so the sixth unit is what makes the coverage statement unconditional rather than attitude-dependent. The **error budget** survives the suite without new tuning, but the reason is the field edge rather than that body diagonal, and the two must not be conflated. The FSS is specified 0.5° (3σ) inside 45° incidence and 2.0° at the 60° edge, and the shipped `SigmaSunWhiteRad` is the **edge** figure. The flight selector orders on *reported σ* with ties to the lowest index, and σ is a two-regime step, so under a tie — the common case, with several units inside 45° — it takes the lowest-indexed unit rather than the best-aimed one, which can sit anywhere out to the 60° edge. The edge figure bounds that; 54.736° would not. So **54.736° is a pure coverage-geometry fact** (the worst-case *best-available* unit, a property of the layout alone) and **the 60° field edge is the accuracy-bounding property of the shipped selector**. The campaign models the flight rule exactly rather than an idealised best-incidence one, and measures the selected unit's worst incidence at the full 60°, which is what makes the budget's edge derivation the load-bearing one. The estimator still never has to be told which unit it is reading. Six of the same part on a 6U is a deliberate simplification and is recorded as one: a cost-optimised vehicle would fly one FSS on the array normal and five coarse photodiode cells, and those report **diode counts**, which needs the onboard counts→vector reconstruction §8.2 names and this push does not implement.

**Selection, not a weighted combination, for the sun.** The onboard rule is the valid, fresh, sun-in-view unit reporting the **smallest realised σ** (§6.4), ties to the lowest index so the choice is deterministic in vehicle build order — the incidence-cosine criterion expressed in the quantity the estimator actually consumes, which stays right if a future suite mixes parts with different accuracy curves where raw incidence would not. It is selection rather than a weighted combination because the sun measurements' error is dominated by a *shared* systematic — the albedo residual and the ephemeris term are common to every unit on the vehicle — which combining cannot average down: a second unit at a worse incidence buys noise reduction on the small term and nothing at all on the large one, while costing a correlation the estimators' white-`R` model has no way to represent. A non-positive or non-finite reported σ is gated with the rest (§9.1) rather than trusted, since it would otherwise win every comparison by being the smallest number in the array. Magnetometers and GNSS keep first-valid-unit priority, honestly: there is one of each, and a combination rule with no redundancy to exercise is untested code.

**The albedo boresight became per unit, which is what makes the handoff meaningful.** `SunAlbedoBoresightsBody` replaces the single `SunAlbedoBoresightBody`: one flat `Vec3F64PerUnit` parameter (`GncMaxUnits × 3` F64), indexed by the selected port, with the **zero vector** meaning "not installed, or mounting not characterised" and taking the uncorrected path. Flat and singular rather than eight named parameters because that would be eight chances for them to disagree about which vector they describe, and F´ parameter serialization has no array-of-arrays the config compiler encodes. Without this the correction would silently stop running on every attitude that moved the Sun off the array normal, and the accuracy campaign would be measuring a suite the vehicle does not fly. The config compiler now cross-checks each slot against that unit's `mounting_quaternion_wxyz` — the same class of silent failure the peak/field-of-view cross-check already guards, one level down, since a wrong boresight *scales* the correction rather than failing it. The peak and field of view stay one value each because they describe the *part*; a mixed suite would need them per unit too, which is another parameter of the same shape rather than a redesign.

**Measured.** The §8.1 campaign gains two cases. `SunSuiteCoversEveryAttitude` checks both claims directly over 2×10⁵ directions rather than inheriting them from the derivation, and separately, on the right quantity each: none uncovered, worst **best-available** incidence 54.70° against the analytic 54.736°, worst **selected** incidence 60.00° at the field edge, 87.9% of directions inside the FSS 45° regime. It also asserts that the selected unit is sometimes worse than the best available, which is the property that would silently disappear if the campaign's selector drifted away from the flight one. `KnowledgeHoldsAcrossSunSensorHandoffs` re-flies the **verifying post-calibration budget** at slew rates (≈29 °/s over 5 s) through the suite, with the truth noise following the sensor's accuracy-vs-incidence curve at whichever unit is selected while both estimators are told the shipped edge constant — exactly what the flight code does. 708 of 800 runs hand off at least once, 1882 handoffs in total, every one of the six units is selected somewhere, and the requirement bounds hold with margin: coarse **2.70°** against 5° (46%) and fine **1.76°** against 3° (41%), against 2.48°/2.25° on the steady-state campaign. Correcting the campaign's selector to the flight rule moved the handoff *counts* (the tie-broken index changes more often than the best-aimed one does) and left the accuracy bounds unchanged to three decimals — which is the expected result rather than a lucky one: the selector takes the **minimum** reported σ, so the σ that reaches the estimator is the same either way, and only the bookkeeping of which unit supplied it differs. The fine bound *improves* across the sweep, which is the accuracy-vs-incidence curve showing through — 88% of the sampled geometries put the Sun inside the FSS's 45° regime, where the true noise is four times tighter than the constant the estimator is weighting with. That last point names the deferred optimisation honestly: the measurement port already carries the realised per-sample σ and the estimator does not consume it, which would need a per-cycle white-σ override on `CoarseAttitudeInput` (the MEKF already takes σ per update). New requirements **REQ-ADET-008** (single-fault-tolerant rate combination), **REQ-ADET-009** (exclusion, reporting, re-admission) and **REQ-ADET-010** (suite coverage and handoff) carry the verification.

On the vehicle, `SitlImuVoting` in `tests/integration/sitl_attitude_tuning_test.cpp` exercises the **identification** path rather than the easy one, and the choice of fault is the point: 0.05 rad/s (2.9 °/s) injected into one of the two units is about six times the pairwise disagreement gate and a tenth of the plausibility limit, so **no per-unit gate can see it** and only the comparison can — which is the fault a two-unit suite exists to handle, and the one a mean would silently split the difference on. A hard-railed unit is the *easier* case, caught by the rate-limit gate before the pair is ever consulted, and is covered in the component harness and `imu_voting_test.cpp` instead. The SITL run asserts `ImuUnitExcluded(1, OUTVOTED)`, **no** `ImuVoteAmbiguous` (the tie-break was available and used), no `AttitudeLost`, no fine-mode demotion — note the circularity that last one would expose, since the filter that identified the fault is the one that then has to survive it — and automatic re-admission on recovery. A second SITL case witnesses the **other** branch on the vehicle rather than only in unit tests: faulting the unit from the first step puts the disagreement in force before the filter has ever published a rate, and the state is self-sustaining because the vote withholds the very rate the filter would need to acquire one. It asserts the refusal is reported **once** (edge-gated over a 30 s run rather than 300 times), that **nothing is latched** — detection is not attribution, and excluding the wrong unit would spend the remaining redundancy on a guess — and that the cost is bounded to the *rate*: the sun and magnetic pairs are untouched by a gyro fault, so TRIAD still acquires and the vehicle keeps an attitude, which is what makes withholding the rate a safe response rather than a self-inflicted outage.
- **Star tracker into the MEKF (user decision, 2026-08-02):** the tracker is fused as an **attitude** measurement, and the finest rung of the ladder is **ST+IMU only** — with at least one valid tracker the sun and magnetic pairs are not fused at all. A **second tracker with a non-parallel boresight** covers the first's about-boresight weak axis (§8.1). Implemented Push 52, below.

**Star trackers, the mode ladder, dual magnetometers (Implemented Push 52).** The second half of §8.2. The reference vehicle grows to **two star trackers and two magnetometers**, the MEKF learns to take a complete attitude as a measurement, the estimator gains a mode *ladder* rather than a fine/coarse binary, and the second tracker is calibrated against the first on orbit. REQ-ADET-007 and REQ-PAY-001 — held at `reviewed` with no verifying artifact since Push 45 because the mode they are stated in was unreachable — are verified, and REQ-ADET-007 is **tightened from 0.05° to 0.03°** on the evidence: **0.021° against 0.03° (30% margin)** and **0.019° against 0.8° (98%)**.

**The MEKF's second update, and why it is not three vector updates.** `Mekf::updateAttitude` (`lib/gnc/mekf`) folds in a complete Body ← ECI solution with `H = [I₃ 0₃]` and a **full 3×3 `R`** (Markley & Crassidis §6.2.4 and §7.1 [markley2014]; Lefferts, Markley & Shuster §III [lefferts1982]). A tracker's output *is* the attitude, so no direction is degenerate and the update is the full 3 DOF; decomposing it into observed star directions would need the star catalogue the vendor does not publish, and feeding the quaternion in as synthetic vectors would double-count the same information and lose the anisotropy. The innovation is the **exact** rotation vector of `q_meas ⊗ q̂⁻¹` taken the short way round (`2·atan2(‖v‖, |q₀|)`, §3.3) — the same reduction `Mekf::nees` uses, so it is the filter's own `δθ` rather than a linearisation that happens to agree for small angles. That matters at acquisition, where a tracker returning after a coast can be tens of degrees out and `2·vec(δq)` would understate it. The gate is its own configured value on **3** degrees of freedom (χ²₃ at 99.9% = 16.27) rather than the vector path's χ²₂: applying a 2-DOF threshold to a 3-DOF statistic gates at ~99.0% instead of ~99.9%, ten times the false-rejection rate, on the one source the finest mode is built around — and a rejection streak there demotes the mode. `R` is a trust boundary and is required symmetric **positive-definite** by a successful Cholesky, for the same reason the seed covariance is: an indefinite `R` makes `S` indefinite and the NIS could come back negative, which a one-sided "too large?" test waves straight through.

**`R`'s anisotropy is the whole reason to carry two trackers**, and it is why only a *boresight* is configured rather than a full mounting. A tracker barely constrains rotation about its own boresight — the identified stars hardly move — so the caller passes `R = σ_xy²(I − b̂b̂ᵀ) + σ_z² b̂b̂ᵀ` in body axes. That closed form is symmetric about the boresight, so the sensor's roll within its own mount cannot affect it, and one 3-vector per unit is the whole configuration. Two units with non-parallel boresights then each carry the other's weak direction. `tests/unit/mekf_test.cpp` pins the algebra directly: adding a second tracker at 90° drops the *first's* about-boresight variance by more than 10×, and the covariance difference is checked as a **matrix inequality** (every eigenvalue non-negative) rather than on a few axes, because a sign error in the rotated `R` would show up in an off-diagonal.

**The king tracker defines the body frame.** One unit is designated the attitude reference: its mounting *is* the body frame, no alignment is estimated for it, and the calibration command refuses it. That removes an unobservable degree of freedom rather than hiding one — two trackers on a structure observe only their *relative* rotation, so an unmodelled common rotation of the assembly is indistinguishable from a rotation of the body frame, and estimating two absolute alignments from tracker data alone is estimating six parameters from three observable ones. What it does **not** do is remove the king's own bias: the payload is mounted against the physical structure, not against the king's optical axis, so that bias is a real knowledge error and it is the floor the accuracy campaign measures. Stating that plainly is the point of the architecture — the alternative silently buries it.

**The alignment calibration (`lib/gnc/st_alignment`, `ST_ALIGN_CAL_*`).** With `q_k` and `q_2` simultaneous solutions, the residual misalignment is the constant `q_rel = q_2 ⊗ q_k⁻¹`, and the calibration estimates it as the **maximum-eigenvalue quaternion average** — the eigenvector of `M = Σ q_rel q_relᵀ` belonging to its largest eigenvalue (Markley, Cheng, Crassidis & Oshman 2007 [markley2007]). That is the right average and not merely a convenient one: arithmetic-mean-then-renormalise is only its first-order approximation and biases with dispersion, and the outer product is invariant to the ±q sign ambiguity, so no sample can cancel another by having been reported with the opposite sign. `M` is a 4×4 accumulator, so the window is **O(1) in its length** — the same property the magnetometer calibration's normal equations have, and for the same reason. Solved with the fixed-size `SelfAdjointEigenSolver<Matrix4d>`, the same choice as `gnc::davenport` and for the same reason. Both quality metrics fall out of the same eigenvalues **exactly**: `λ_max = Σ cos²(θᵢ/2)` gives the RMS residual `2·sqrt(1 − λ_max/N)` without revisiting a sample, and the normalised gap `(λ_max − λ₂)/N` is the consistency metric. One numerical consequence is stated rather than discovered: the square root amplifies round-off, so on noise-free data the residual floors at ~4 milliarcsec rather than zero — six orders below any real gate, but a caller must not read `residual == 0` as the perfect-fit condition.

**The eigen-gap gate is not a geometry gate, and conflating the two would mis-describe the whole calibration.** Attitude pairs have *no* degenerate geometry — one pair determines all three parameters, at any attitude — so there is no analogue of TRIAD's near-parallel refusal here and no coverage gate to get wrong. A window taken with the vehicle parked is as valid as one taken through a tumble, which is the structural difference from the magnetometer fit and is pinned as its own test. What the gap detects is a *fault*: a unit delivering solutions that do not sit at a fixed rotation from the king's — a mis-identified star field, a stale or cross-wired solution, a mounting that is moving. Refusing there is refusing to average a rotation that does not exist. There is also deliberately **no "must beat the uncalibrated fit" gate** of the kind the magnetometer calibration carries: an ellipsoid fit can converge on a worse sensor model, whereas an alignment estimate is a mean of a quantity that is either constant (and the mean is right) or not (and the gap catches it). Simultaneity is the measurement — a pair enters only on a cycle where both units solved, because at 0.1 °/s a one-cycle skew is already 36 arcsec, comparable to the effect being measured — so the window is counted in **accepted pairs** with a self-close deadline, exactly as the magnetometer window is counted in accepted samples. The commanded flow is the Push 46 pattern verbatim: `ST_ALIGN_CAL_START(unit, sampleCount)` / `ABORT` / `CLEAR(unit)`, parameters read at command time so a missing one refuses the *command*, the fit on **uncorrected** readings so an applied correction never refits itself, one application point between unit gathering and every consumer, and nothing written to `ParameterDb` — the correction does not survive a reboot until §23.6 lands, which is documented rather than automated.

**The mode ladder, and what "demoted" means.** With at least one tracker accepted, the sun and magnetic pairs are **not** fused. They are two orders of magnitude wider, so folding them in can only pull the solution away from the trackers, and the filter's white-`R` model has no way to represent the systematic floor that makes them wide — it would average down an offset that does not average down and report a covariance tighter than the truth for it. What the demotion buys is not merely avoiding that: it is **observability**. A source the filter is updating from cannot be checked against the filter, because the residual is small *because* the update made it small. Demoted, the same measurement becomes a monitor, and a sun sensor that has drifted 20° becomes an event on the ground's console instead of a slightly worse solution. Two monitors run on the demoted pairs against the fine solution, and a third — the sun-sensor **cross-unit** check — runs in every mode, because it compares two sensors against each other and needs no attitude at all. That third one closes a gap the Push 51 selector left open: the flight rule takes the smallest *reported* σ, so a unit that is confidently wrong wins and nothing else notices. Detection needs no attitude; *resolution* does, and is gated on the monitor having already alerted, so a single noisy sample can never move the selection. Nothing is latched — the override is re-decided each cycle from current evidence, so a recovering unit simply stops being overridden. A monitor that cannot run resets its streak but does **not** clear an existing alert: "we stopped looking" is not "it recovered", and clearing on absence would let a faulted sensor close its own alert by dropping out.

**A rung change is not a demotion, and the distinction is load-bearing.** Moving between ST+IMU and SS+MAG leaves the filter's state and covariance intact and the published solution valid throughout; it is reported as `FineSourceChanged`, not `FineModeDemoted`, and produces no `AttitudeLost`. What changes is how fast the covariance grows from there — about two orders of magnitude — which is why a consumer with a knowledge requirement (a payload, REQ-PAY-001) gates on `FineSource` and not merely on `attitudeValid`. Promotion also changes: **a tracker seeds the filter on its own**, from its own solution and its own `R`, without the vector pairs and without requiring a valid coarse solution underneath. The coarse-floor precondition is a good rule when the seed comes from the same two wide sources the floor is built from and the wrong rule against a tracker, which *is* the better source — requiring it would make the top rung unreachable in eclipse and at cold start, the cases a tracker exists to cover.

**Dual magnetometers, and the vote that is now shared.** The reference vehicle grows a second magnetometer, combined by the same ladder the IMUs use. Rather than copy that machinery, the Push 51 voter was split: the *policy* — plausibility ladder, per-axis median at three or more, pairwise identification with its margin and confirmation gates, exclusion latch, criterion-matched re-admission — moved into `lib/gnc/unit_voting`, and `imu_voting` and `mag_voting` became thin wrappers supplying the two things that actually differ. That is the point of the split rather than a tidiness exercise: a divergence between the two votes' policies would be a defect, and one implementation cannot diverge from itself. What differs for a magnetometer is, first, the **plausibility gate** — the measured magnitude against the onboard IGRF magnitude, as a ratio band. This is the natural physical test and it costs nothing new, because the vehicle already evaluates IGRF-14 at its own position every cycle to build the magnetic reference; it is *attitude-free*, so it works in Safe mode and at cold start; and it is strictly better than a fixed full-scale check, because it tracks the field from ~22 µT to ~52 µT over an orbit instead of admitting everything below saturation. A cycle with no modelled field is **refused** rather than run with the band disabled — a vote whose only surviving gate is finiteness would admit the railed reading the band exists to catch. Second, the **identification reference** is the modelled field rotated into body axes, and it carries a *quality* condition rather than a validity flag: a flag cannot tell a 0.5° solution from a 10° one, and a 10° attitude error mispredicts a 30 µT field by ~5 µT — the disagreement gate itself. Third, the **cost of refusing is smaller and deliberately so**: an unattributable magnetometer disagreement loses one of two vector pairs, where the IMU equivalent loses the body rate, so the vehicle keeps propagating and keeps acquiring. The escalation horizon is *shared* with the IMU vote rather than duplicated, because how long the ground should wait before a persistent refusal reaches a console is a property of the operations concept, not of the sensor.

**A fourth validity gate, and the deployment's second config override.** The star-tracker fusion set (king unit, the two sigmas, the per-unit boresights, the three monitor thresholds and their shared persistence) is validated **separately** from the coarse, fine and albedo sets, and the cost structure is why: a missing value caps the ladder at SS+MAG and the vehicle flies at REQ-ADET-006 accuracy — a real loss for a payload, not for safety — so it emits `StConfigInvalid` and carries on. `MekfAttNisGate` is the exception and rides in the *fine* set, because `MekfConfig::isValid` cannot build a filter without it. Eighteen new parameters take the reference vehicle to **55**, comfortably inside the 64 `PRMDB_NUM_DB_ENTRIES` Push 46 raised. The three new commands, however, pushed the deployment past `CMD_DISPATCHER_DISPATCH_TABLE_SIZE = 150` — an `FW_ASSERT` during topology setup, after 150 perfectly healthy `OpCodeRegistered` events — so `flight/config/` gained a second `CONFIGURATION_OVERRIDES` header raising it to 256, on the same reasoning and in the same place as the parameter-database one.

**Measured, and read honestly.** The campaign (`tests/unit/attitude_accuracy_mc_test.cpp`, 800 runs) models the anisotropy in each unit's own frame, draws each unit's fixed bias once per run from the datasheet's *bound* (isotropic direction, magnitude uniform in `[0, B]`, so per-axis 1σ = `B/3` = 20.4 arcsec), and flies the alignment calibration through the **real** accumulator rather than subtracting a stipulated residual. Dual-tracker **0.021°** 3σ, median 0.009°; single-tracker **0.024°**; cross-boresight **0.019°** against REQ-PAY-001's 0.8°. Three things about those numbers are worth stating rather than celebrating. **First, the second tracker's benefit is real but modest, and is measured as a paired comparison** — the same run flown twice, differing only in whether unit 1 is fused, so it is not two independent samples. Dual wins on **65.0% of 800 runs** against a 50 ± 1.8% null. The datasheet asymmetry is ~6× on the *noise* terms, but the isotropic bias dominates the cross-boresight budget and compresses the ratio the fusion sees to 1.8×; the geometric benefit is real and is pinned in the filter's own algebra, but on the norm metric it is spent against a floor the trackers share. **Second, that floor is the king's bias**, and the campaign asserts the measured median stays *above* it — a result below it would mean the campaign is averaging down a systematic that does not average down, which is the one way this could be wrong in the flattering direction. **Third, the earlier §8.1 estimate of ~115 arcsec for a single tracker was pessimistic** because it added a 3σ noise RSS to a bias *bound* as if they were the same kind of quantity; the measured single-tracker figure is 86 arcsec. **The tightening is enacted at 0.03°, not at the committed 0.02°.** The measured bound sits just *above* 0.02° at 0.021°, so enacting that would make the requirement fail outright; the smallest threshold carrying the declared 20% margin on this bound is **0.03°**, which lands at 30%. Like REQ-ADET-005/006 it is conditioned — post-calibration parameter set plus a valid inter-tracker alignment — with king-only recorded as the degraded floor at 0.024°, since a single tracker clears the old 0.05° but not 0.03° with margin. Worth noting how little separates 0.020° from 0.021° — the two are the same campaign before and after the RNG substreams were separated to make the single/dual comparison paired. A committed threshold that moves with a test-harness refactor is too close to its own measurement, which is the concrete reason not to chase 0.02°. Going *below* 0.03° belongs with a decision about whether the king's bias can be reduced at all: a ground-calibrated tracker mounting, or an absolute alignment against a payload-derived reference, are the levers, and neither is in the current design.
- **Commanded sun-sensor calibration (sequenced after tracker fusion):** on-orbit SS bias/alignment calibration needs an attitude reference better than the sun sensor itself — pre-tracker, the filter's attitude is SS-dependent and the fit would be circular. With the tracker fused, a commanded collection regresses SS residuals against tracker-derived attitude and writes the correction through the parameter path.

### 8.3 Orbit Determination & Propagation
- **Onboard MEKF OD** from GNSS-sim measurements (biases/noise), onboard force model, **with self-covariance propagation**. GNSS inputs are converted from GPS time/ECEF to TAI/ECI before the inertial-frame filter runs (§3.2).
- **Multi-object propagation** of up to N secondaries (§11.2).
- **Batch least-squares** estimator (ground/analysis) for orbit fit and validation.
- **SGP4** TLE propagation; **CCSDS OEM** ephemeris generation as a data product.
- GNSS pseudo-noise: GNSS satellite states from TLE propagation, pseudorange construction, receiver clock and ionosphere error contributions.

**The onboard filter — implemented, `lib/gnc/orbit_od.{hpp,cpp}`.** The orbit half of `EstimatedState` (§8.0): a 6-state error-state EKF on ECI `[δr; δv]`, flight-safe (fixed-size Eigen, no heap, no exceptions, bounded loops, return codes checked, finiteness-guarded output; no F´ types and no I/O), so the F´ component that ships it is a thin wrapper. The error state is **additive** — a translational state has no group structure and no unit-norm constraint, so the "MEKF" of the bullet above means *one block of the same 15-state error-state filter*, not a multiplicative parameterisation of position; that word belongs to §8.1's attitude error and nothing here reuses it.

**The force model is coarse but no longer J2-only: an 8×8 EGM2008 truncation + a single-band exponential atmosphere.** That is still far below the truth sim's 200×200 EGM2008 / NRLMSIS / third-body / SRP stack, and the gap is the design (§18, `sim/CLAUDE.md`).

The filter originally flew closed-form two-body + J2, and the argument for it was that GNSS dominates the solution so a better field buys nothing. *That argument holds for the estimate and is unchanged*: with 1 Hz fixes at ~1 m the steady-state error is receiver-bound and no force model moves it. It does not hold for the two things the dynamics is actually load-bearing for — **coasting through an outage**, and **onboard ephemeris prediction** (§20 pass planning, eclipse prediction, maneuver targeting) — where the J2 truncation *was* the whole error budget: 3.17 m over the 300 s coast horizon against a 32×32 truth, and 14.3 m over 699 s against GMAT.

The objection the original argument raised against a table was cost, and the measured cost overruled it. 8×8 is **45 coefficient pairs compiled in** (`lib/gnc/egm2008_low_degree.hpp`, ~1 kB of `.rodata`) — not an uploaded table, so no staleness, validity or CRC story — evaluated by the **unnormalised Cunningham V/W recursion** (Montenbruck & Gill §3.2.4) with `(N+2)²` doubles of stack scratch and no precomputed tables. It is not in the 10 Hz GNC cycle: the propagation runs at the fix rate and its sub-steps. The coefficients are derived on the ground from the committed ICGEM `.gfc` by `tools/gravity/cxxtable.py` (§3.7: the reference datum stays the `.gfc`), de-normalised there rather than in flight, with `tests/tools/test_gravity_cxxtable.py` regenerating and comparing every run so the two cannot drift.

*Why a third implementation of a field the repo already evaluates twice.* `sim/world/gravity_field` carries heap-allocated coefficient and normalisation tables sized at construction and a runtime-settable degree — correct for a 200×200 truth model, disallowed on the flight path (§3.6). Templating it to serve both would force the sim's case into a 20301-entry `std::array` and rewrite the model GMAT already certified. The safety comes from cross-validation instead: `tests/unit/geopotential_test.cpp` pins the flight evaluator against the sim's independent normalised-Gottlieb engine at **every degree 2..8** over a position spread including both poles (worst 5e-9 relative), and the golden test pins it against **GMAT's EGM96** at matched degree. V/W is safe here and not at 200 because its sectoral terms grow like `(2n−1)!!`; `cxxtable.py` refuses to emit a table above degree 20 so the evaluator can never be handed coefficients it cannot carry.

What survives from the original argument is its third point, and it still does the real work: the remaining truncation is absorbed as **process noise sized from its own measurement**, so improving the field re-derives `q_a` rather than leaving a now-conservative value in place. Degree 0 restores the two-body path and `zonal_j2` the closed-form J2 one — how the golden test compares this propagator against GMAT at matched fidelity — and the two paths are mutually exclusive by config validation, since a config setting both describes the same physics twice with one silently ignored.

Two details of the model are not conveniences. **The field is evaluated in ECEF, per integration sub-step.** A geopotential is defined in the Earth-fixed frame and ECI is not it. For the zonal fallback path only the pole *direction* matters, since a zonal field is axisymmetric — but even there ECI must not be used naively: precession and nutation separate the two poles by ~0.36° by 2026, tilting the J2 bulge by that angle at ~100 m per LEO revolution, which is the defect the truth sim was caught with by GMAT cross-validation. The harmonic path cannot take that shortcut at all, because a **tesseral** term is fixed to the rotating Earth. So the full ECI↔ECEF rotation comes from the same `frames::eci_ecef` reduction everything else uses (REQ-CONV-002), resolved **per sub-step** rather than once per propagation: Earth turns 0.004°/s, so holding one rotation across a 60 s step would smear the tesserals by 0.25° of longitude. Within a sub-step it is held across the four RK4 stages, where 6e-5 rad of rotation mis-orients a ~1e-5 m/s² term by ~6e-10 m/s². And **drag acts on the atmosphere-relative velocity** `v − ω⊕ × r`: at LEO the transport term is ~465 m/s against 7.7 km/s, i.e. ~12% of the drag magnitude and a real cross-track component if dropped. The drag band is one band of the *same* Vallado Table 8-4 piecewise fit the truth sim interpolates, chosen at the operating altitude, so the onboard/truth atmosphere difference is exactly "one band versus the whole table" — a bounded, attributable approximation rather than two unrelated atmospheres.

**Propagation and Φ.** Classical RK4 over sub-steps of at most `max_step_s`, bounded at compile time by `kMaxSubsteps`; `OrbitOdConfig::isValid` refuses any config where that bound could truncate a legitimate step, so the failure is at construction rather than four orbits into a run. The covariance goes `P = ΦPΦᵀ + Q` per sub-step with `Φ = I + Fh + ½F²h²`, the second-order truncation of `exp(Fh)` — the next term is `O((‖F‖h)³) ≈ 2e-10` at LEO and `h ≤ 1 s`. **The Jacobian is numerical, by central differences, and that is a choice with a stated price.** The analytic alternative (a one-line two-body gradient, a seven-term J2 gradient, a drag gradient carrying the density derivative) is a *second implementation of the force model* that has to be kept in step with the first by hand; a numerical Jacobian differentiates whatever the propagator actually computes and cannot drift from it when a term is added. The accuracy question that raises is settled by test rather than argument: it is pinned against the closed-form `−μ/r³(I − 3r̂r̂ᵀ)`. Cost is twelve acceleration evaluations per sub-step, against a 10 Hz budget.

**Process noise, and where the number comes from.** `Q` is the discretised continuous white-noise-acceleration model, cross-terms kept (the position and velocity error from one unmodelled acceleration are the same error seen twice). `q_a` is set by matching the CWNA position spread `σ_r(T) = √(q_a T³/3)` to the *measured* divergence between this force model and the full-fidelity one at the coast horizon: `q_a = 3 δr(T)²/T³`. For the reference vehicle (12 kg, 400 km, 51.6°) the measurement against the GMAT-validated truth sim at **32×32** gravity is **1.28 m over 300 s**, carried at the measurement, giving **`q_a = 1.8e-7 m²/s³`**. It was carried at 1.8 m (~40% margin, `q_a = 3.6e-7`) until the OD Monte Carlo (§23.2, `analysis/od`) measured that margin as covariance pessimism: campaign NEES 4.22 against a 95% floor of 4.83, all of it in velocity, and consistent (5.28) only with the margin removed. The headroom was there so the CI fence on the measurement does not flap with the epoch, and it now lives on that fence alone (`kTruncationFenceM`, 1.5 m) — the two used to be one constant, and the fence's slack was reaching the filter as a 30% velocity-σ over-budget. The measured growth is still quadratic — 0.0138 m at 30 s against 1.280 m at 300 s — which is the direct evidence that what remains is a *systematic acceleration*, so the white fit is documented as an approximation matched at the horizon: conservative inside it, optimistic beyond it. That asymmetry is the reason the horizon exists rather than a reason to distrust it.

*Both the number and its reference moved when the 8×8 field landed, and the two must not be confused.* The old figure was 3.59 m against an **8×8** truth, which the onboard model now matches — an 8×8-vs-8×8 comparison measures two implementations of one field and reports 0.045 m, which would be a passing test that asserts nothing. The truth degree was therefore raised to 32×32 rather than the smaller number banked. On one arc against that harder reference, both models: closed-form J2 **3.17 m**, the 8×8 field **1.28 m** — a 2.5× reduction, like-for-like. `CoarseModelTruncationOverTheCoastHorizon` flies both every run and fails if the field ever stops winning, so the justification for carrying a coefficient table on the flight side is a live gate rather than a claim in this document.

**Fix latency.** A receiver's solution is valid at its *measurement* epoch but reaches the FSW a fix latency later — tens of milliseconds of internal processing plus bus transport and scheduling. At 7.6 km/s that is ~7.6 m of along-track position per millisecond, which makes it the **largest single error this filter can carry**: an order above the receiver's own ~1 m noise and two above the geopotential truncation the harmonic field was added to remove. `config/hardware/gnss/novatel_oem7600.yaml` now carries `fix_latency_s: 0.05` (the datasheet's 20 ms measurement latency plus transport) and `sim/sensors/gnss` realises it as a delay line, delivering the newest solution at least that old, tagged at its own measurement epoch — a model that stamped on delivery would hide the error and make it uncorrectable rather than merely present.

The correction is applied to the **measurement**, not the filter. A fix arriving behind the filter's epoch — the normal case, since the GNC cycle has propagated in the meantime — has its reported PVT advanced to the filter's epoch on the receiver's *own reported velocity* plus the onboard acceleration, `r += vτ + ½aτ²`, `v += aτ`, with `R` inflated by `(σ_v τ)²` for the velocity error that integrates into position across the advance. This is the benign corner of Bar-Shalom §5.6's out-of-sequence-measurement problem: the measurement determines the whole state and carries its own velocity, so the linear term is *measured* rather than modelled and the residual is `O(τ³·jerk)` — micrometres at τ ≤ 0.2 s. It is the solution-domain analogue of the signal-transit-time correction Kim et al. (2025) apply to navigation-solution measurements. Retrodicting the *filter* instead is textbook-exact and not worth a stored prior and two extra propagations per fix to fix an error the measurement's own velocity already resolves to micrometres. The correction is bounded by `max_fix_latency_s` (0.2 s, 4× the receiver's figure); past it a fix is a clock fault and is refused, and a latent fix *without* a velocity is refused too, since borrowing the filter's velocity would fold the filter's error into a measurement required to be independent of it. The realised latency is telemetered on every fix, because a latency that climbs is invisible in the residuals precisely because the filter corrects for it.

**Measurements.** Ingest owns the §3.2/§6.2 conversion before anything inertial runs (REQ-CONV-001): `TAI = GPS + 19 s`, and the full ECEF→ECI **state** transform — position *and* velocity, since a rotation alone cannot transform a state. A fix whose epoch the uploaded EOP table does not cover is **refused**, not converted on an extrapolated Earth orientation. Position and velocity fold in as two **sequential** Joseph-form 3-row updates rather than one 6-row batch: the receiver draws them independently so `R` is block-diagonal and the two are algebraically identical, but sequential keeps every matrix a fixed 3×3 or 6×3, gives each its own NIS and gate, and lets a fix without a velocity report take the position path unchanged. `R` is the receiver's **own reported per-fix accuracy**, and it is anisotropic: `sim/sensors/gnss` realises position error split horizontal/vertical in the local geodetic frame and carries the realised σ, so the filter reconstructs that covariance in the basis it was drawn in and rotates it into ECI rather than flattening it. `R` is nonetheless treated as **white**, and real GNSS error is not — ionosphere, broadcast-orbit and clock error are common-mode and correlated over minutes (§6.2). The truth model is white too today, so the campaign below validates the filter against the model it was given; when the correlated component lands, this filter needs an inflated `R` or a state-augmented bias, and that is stated rather than pretended.

**Coast horizon: 300 s, and the solution is dropped at it.** Sized from the outage modes the receiver model actually has — the reference NovAtel OEM7600's 34 s cold-start time-to-first-fix and 0.5 s reacquisition, both in `config/hardware/gnss` — at ~9× the dominant one, which covers a cold start plus a missed reacquisition plus a run of dropped fixes, while staying short enough that the model error over it is comparable to one fix's noise. Past it the solution is declared invalid **and dropped**, and the next fix re-acquires whole. That is the *coarse attitude* estimator's discipline, not the MEKF's, and the difference is deliberate: the MEKF retains past its horizon because there is a converged gyro bias worth keeping, whereas here the entire state is position and velocity, a GNSS fix determines both outright and better than any coasted prior, and past the horizon the model error is a systematic that a stale prior would only drag the fresh fix toward.

**Refusals name themselves.** `OrbitOdRefusal` distinguishes unconfigured, uninitialised, a non-finite fix, a fix outside the configured geocentric-radius band (§9.1 — wire data is not trusted, and an unchecked position poisons the propagation while every validity flag still reads true), a non-positive σ, a **non-increasing fix epoch**, a step beyond `max_dt_s`, a failed frame conversion, a seed attempted without a velocity, a NIS-gated measurement, and an internal fault. The stuck-clock guard is on the *fix* epoch rather than only on the propagation, because a re-presented fix at the epoch the filter already sits at would skip propagation entirely and fold the same measurement in twice, shrinking the covariance on information already used. Gates are written as accept ranges `0 ≤ NIS ≤ gate`, since a one-sided test waves a negative NIS — an indefinite covariance — straight through. An internal fault drops the solution *without* clearing the rejection count. The product goes into `EstimatedState` via the overloaded `gnc::writeToEstimatedState`: position, velocity, their validity, and the position/velocity covariance blocks **including their cross terms**, written only when the solution is valid; attitude, rates, biases and `mode` are left alone, since `mode` names the *attitude* mode and an orbit filter has no business overwriting it.

**Verification — two questions, not one.** *(a) The propagator is a correctness question, and GMAT answers it.* `tests/golden/orbit_od_golden_test.cpp` runs the onboard propagator against the same committed fixture the truth sim is held to, at **matched fidelity**: worst position residual **3.5 mm** on `two_body` (identical to the truth sim's own 3.5 mm, i.e. entirely GMAT's integrator) and **1.03 cm** on `zonal_j2`, against 5 cm and 50 cm bands. Standing it up immediately found two real constant-pairing defects, both of the kind that conserve energy perfectly and so survive every self-consistency check: `kJ2` had been paired with WGS84's semi-major axis rather than the 6378136.3 m radius the coefficient was solved with (9.1 cm → 7.0 cm), and with WGS84's GM rather than the geopotential's own (7.0 cm → 1.0 cm) — the second confirmed by ablation, since the same GM swap *degrades* the file-less `two_body` case from 3.5 mm to 6.1 cm, which is what proves it is the fixture's behaviour and not a fudge. Both constants now live paired in `constants::gravity`. The same test's LEO cases used to record a *truncation* (14.3 m at 699 s for `iss_leo`, 12.9 m at 600 s for `sso_leo`) under an asserted bound. They no longer can: the fixture flies `gravity_degree: 8` and so does the filter, so the comparison is matched fidelity and a bound on it would bound nothing. It was repurposed rather than left to pass vacuously, and what it measures now is stronger — GMAT's **EGM96** to degree 8 against Polaris's **EGM2008** to degree 8, by completely different code, agreeing to **1.2 cm** (`iss_leo`) and **0.4 cm** (`sso_leo`), which is the EGM96/EGM2008 coefficient separation itself and not a propagator difference. That is an independent-tool validation of the flight evaluator's recursion, its de-normalised table and its ECEF frame handling at once, and it is the check a comparison against `sim/world/gravity_field` cannot supply, since a defect shared between sim and flight would pass that one. The J2-only model still flies the identical arcs in the same test, reproducing 14.26 m and 12.95 m, so the ~1000× improvement stays anchored to an independent tool. *(b) Truth versus estimation is a statistical question, and GMAT cannot referee it* — it has no filter. `tests/unit/orbit_od_test.cpp` Monte-Carlos the filter (120 seeded runs × 200 s) against a truth perturbed by exactly the `Q_d` the filter budgets and measured with exactly the σ it is told: **sample NEES 6.03 against a state dimension of 6, sample NIS 3.01 against a measurement dimension of 3**, and a gate rejection rate of 0.11% against the 0.1% a 99.9% gate should produce. That last figure is the other half of the claim and is asserted too: a covariance that is optimistic passes a naive accuracy test and shows up later as innovation gating rejecting *good* measurements. Also pinned analytically: circular-orbit closure over a full period, two-body energy conservation, drag dissipating at the closed-form `−½ρBv³` rate, the J2 **nodal regression** matching `Ω̇ = −(3/2)n J₂(Rₑ/p)²cos i` to 2% with the complementary polar-orbit null, and the full refusal matrix including the stuck clock (asserted on the *covariance*, not the return code).

**On estimating drag and receiver clock — decided, and decided against for now.** Both were considered as filter states and neither earns one at this architecture. *Receiver clock bias and drift are not observable here at all*: the measurement is a **PVT solution**, and the receiver has already solved its own 4-state least-squares and removed the clock from both the position and the time tag. Clock states earn their keep only in a **tightly-coupled** filter on raw pseudorange/Doppler — which is exactly why NASA's GEONS carries bias, drift *and* drift-rate, and why it can hold a solution with fewer than four visible SVs. Right feature, wrong ordering: it arrives with the raw-measurement path already owed below, not before it. *A drag coefficient is the wrong state even where it is observable*: `C_d` is not separable from atmospheric density error or from `A/m`, so the POD literature estimates it piecewise as a lumped absorber rather than as a physical coefficient; and drag is ~1e-7 m/s² at 400 km, millimetres over the coast horizon and three orders below the geopotential term. Estimating it would fix the third-largest error. The correct dynamics-side augmentation, when one is wanted, is **3 empirical/DMC accelerations in RTN as first-order Gauss-Markov states** (Tapley §4.9; Montenbruck & Gill §8.2), which absorb geopotential truncation, drag and SRP together — 6 → 9 states, and cheaper to reach *after* the field improvement above rather than instead of it.

**The F´ component — `OrbitEstimator`, implemented (Push 65).** A passive component, **member 0** of the 10 Hz GNC rate group — ahead of the attitude estimator, so the position the attitude references are evaluated at is *this* cycle's solution rather than last cycle's. Per cycle it takes the freshest valid fix off its `gnssIn` port array (the receiver's own reported per-fix accuracies now ride the `GnssMeas` port and the SITL wire, because `R` is built from them), looks the EOP up from `OnboardTables` at the *fix* epoch, and either ingests the fix or propagates to the cycle epoch, then publishes an `OrbitEstimate` — ECI position and velocity, the covariance's position/velocity σ, the solution age and its **validity** — on `orbitStateOut`, every cycle, valid or not. The attitude estimator consumes it on `orbitStateIn` and *only* there: the receiver no longer reaches the attitude component at all, the §9.1 geocentric-radius plausibility band moved to this component with the fix it gates, and the attitude side keeps only the gate a port cannot promise (finiteness) plus a **consume-once** latch, so a producer that stops running leaves position unavailable rather than a stale vector wearing a valid flag. The practical consequence is the one §8.1 waited on: a receiver outage costs the magnetic reference only once *this* filter has dropped its solution at the coast horizon, and the SITL fault matrix (§23.1.1) now carries the GNSS-outage rows — a 60 s outage costs no reference and no attitude; a 330 s one drops the orbit solution on schedule, costs the magnetic pair, and re-seeds whole when fixes return (REQ-ODP-007). Tuning is the whole `OrbitOdConfig` as no-default F´ parameters read behind one validity gate (`GeopotentialDegree`/`Order`, the drag band, `AccelPsdM2PerS3`, the two NIS gates, `MaxCoastS`, `MaxDtS`, `MaxStepS`, `MaxFixLatencyS`, `MinRadiusM`/`MaxRadiusM`); μ and the reference radius are the geopotential constants and are not parameters, and degree 0 falls back to closed-form J2 rather than to two-body — the flight never flies a point mass. **The fix-latency pairing is checked now**: `tools/configc` refuses a vehicle whose `MaxFixLatencyS` is below the catalogued `fix_latency_s` of the receiver it flies (§19.4), which is the cross-check §8.3 recorded as owed with this seam. Refusals surface as `FixRefused(unit, reason)` with the library's own `OrbitOdRefusal` names mirrored one-to-one into an FPP enum, and a numeric fault as `OrbitFilterFault`; `OrbitSeeded`/`OrbitSolutionDropped` bracket the solution's life, and `OD_RESET` starts it over.

**What Ceresoli et al. (2025) taught, and what it did not (Push 70).** A read of the PoliMi loosely-coupled GNSS/INS error-state filter [ceresoli2025] against this section: their frame reduction (IAU 2006/2000A vs simplified series, 40 m 1σ horizontal on the CPNc series), latency handling (a mean-delay Euler advance; 150 m uncompensated) and Joseph-form update are all things this filter already does more exactly (SOFA-derived reduction with TAI-argument EOP, per-fix advance on the fix's own tag, Joseph form, `q_a` from a measured truncation). Three things were worth taking, and were. **(1) A non-gravitational acceleration input.** Their one real outage result — a burn during an outage at 5 km told vs 9 km blind — is the burn executor's commanded thrust reaching the filter: `OrbitOd::propagate` takes an optional ECI acceleration with a 1σ magnitude, adds it to the force model over the step and its σ² to the process noise over the same step; the F´ `OrbitEstimator` latches `NonGravAccel` from `BurnExecutor` (§17), fresh within `MaxAccelAgeS`, invalid meaning "no thrust known" and never "the last thrust". Measured on the topology, same 60 s / 2.5 m/s burn 260 s into a 900 s outage: **26 m told against 1374 m blind** at the outage's end; the MC campaign's `burn_outage_fed`/`burn_outage_blind` (0.03 m/s² for 120 s inside a 15-min outage): 18 m against 7.2 km; a burn while tracking is absorbed at the receiver's noise (0.13 m, refusals at the false-alarm rate). **(2) Two coast horizons instead of one.** Their 20-min coast on a J2 model was 3 km; on the 8×8 model here it is **7.6 m** with the covariance still consistent (NEES 0.97 against 6) — and the old policy dropped the solution at 300 s and cost the attitude estimator its magnetic reference, which tolerates a kilometre, for no physical reason. The solution is now **FINE** through `MaxCoastS`, **DEGRADED** through `MaxDegradedCoastS` (1800 s; the coasted prediction with the grown covariance, quality on the port), dropped past that; consumers gate on `posSigmaM` against their own tolerance (`MaxPositionSigmaM`, 20 km on the attitude estimator) rather than on the fine horizon; fixes returning inside the degraded horizon are absorbed by update on the grown covariance, not by re-seed. Measured: a 1200 s outage coasts to **2.2 m**, the reference is kept throughout, one seed. This also moved the spoof residual: a 5 km spoof that outlasts the fine horizon is now refused for as long as it lasts and truth is accepted on return — the re-seed-onto-the-lie needs a spoof longer than the degraded horizon. **(3) A ground seed** — `OD_SEED_STATE(epoch, r, v, σ)`, refused when older than the degraded horizon or ahead of the latency bound — for a long outage or a dead receiver (their TLE fallback without the TLE; SGP4 ingestion stays with Phase 6's multi-object work). And **(4)** the receiver model gained delivery-latency jitter (`fix_latency_jitter_s`, 5 ms on the OEM7600) so the filter's per-fix advance is *tested* invisible (0.10 m worst error at 50 ± 5 ms) rather than assumed. Not taken: the loosely-coupled INS mechanisation in the nominal case (their own result: the filter "strictly follows the GNSS output"), and the accelerometer as the acceleration source — a 270 µg bias integrated over a coast is worse than no input, so that path waits on a bias state and a burn-active gate; the executor's commanded thrust is the source now, with `ThrustKnowledgeFrac` as its σ.

**The NESC navigation-filter best practices, applied (Push 71).** A read of NASA/TP-2018-219822 *Navigation Filter Best Practices* [carpenter2018] and its NESC Technical Bulletin 20-03 summary [dennehy2020] against this section and §8.1. What the filters already did as the TP prescribes: EKF on a Cartesian ECI state in SI, TAI internally with no discontinuous scale (§6.3), Joseph-form sequential updates with a two-sided NIS gate (§2.3, §9.1), a `Q` derived from a continuous PSD per sub-step so a rate change does not invalidate the tuning (the §2.2.3 trap), a state transition that is Lear's Method C run far inside its 16 s bound (§2.2.2), the latency correction (§3.1), and the NEES/NIS campaign that is bulletin item (a). What was missing was Ch. 9 — the operability the TP's authors call the practices that kept "dozens of missions … without a failure ever attributed to an EKF" — and it is now on both filters, each entry point citing its section: **(g)** a parameter upload **re-tunes the running filter in place** (`OrbitOd::retune`, `Mekf::retune`; TP §9.3) — before this, changing a gate rebuilt the filter and threw the converged solution away, and on the attitude side *any* estimator parameter demoted the fine mode and lost the gyro bias; a bad upload now warns and leaves the last valid set in force rather than making a running filter inert; **(f)** `OD_REINIT_COV` / `ATT_REINIT_COV` **re-open the covariance without touching the state** (TP §9.2) — the mild remedy for a "smug" filter editing good measurements, which until now needed a reset (and, for the OD, a new fix or an uplinked state); **(d)** an uplinkable three-way **`ACCEPT` / `INHIBIT` / `FORCE` policy per measurement type** (TP §9.1; `gnc/measurement_policy.hpp`, U8 parameters `Position/VelocityMeasMode`, `Sun/Mag/StMeasMode`) — inhibit is the ground's tool against a source the voting has not caught (and withholds it from *both* attitude chains, since it is a statement about the source), force is the recovery from a gate lock without a reset, and a forced update is counted apart (`forcedCount`, `FixesForced`, `MekfForced`) so it is never read as a consistent one; force does **not** override a negative or non-finite NIS, which is the covariance and not the residual; **(e)** a **backup ephemeris** (TP §9.2) — since `OrbitOd` is a plain value, the component keeps a *copy* seeded from a FINE solution every `BackupPeriodS` (600 s, a third of the degraded horizon) and drives it with the same `propagate` and the same thrust input, never a fix; `OD_RESTART_FROM_BACKUP` makes it the solution with no uplink (measured: back on the truth to < 1 m after a commanded drop), and `BackupDivergenceM` is the independent comparator the TP asks for. Two of the TP's remaining recommendations are declined with the reason on record: **underweighting** (Ch. 4) compensates the second-order term of a nonlinear measurement, and the GNSS (`H = [I 0]`, `[0 I]`) and star-tracker (`H = [I 0]`) models are linear, while for the sun/magnetic vectors ½·tr(H_kk P) ≈ δθ²/2 ≈ 1e-3 rad at a 3° post-coast error is an order below the 1e-2 rad σ; and the **UDU factorisation** (Ch. 7) is not worth the rewrite for two 6-state double-precision filters with a Joseph update and explicit symmetrisation — what *is* taken from Ch. 7 is the definiteness check it gets for free from `D`, done here as an LDLᵀ of `P` every cycle (`covarianceHealthy`, `CovarianceHealthy` / `FineCovarianceHealthy`, an edge EVR when it fails) so an indefinite covariance is caught between measurements rather than only when the next innovation returns a negative NIS. One more of the TP's rules produced a test rather than code: §6.3 says a misapplied leap second must not be able to affect the filter, and this filter is TAI-clean except that the ECEF→ECI conversion of a fix reaches UT1 through ΔAT — a table stale by one leap rotates the Earth 15 arcsec (~500 m at LEO). Measured: a tracking filter refuses such fixes on the gate (innovation 490 m, solution untouched); a **cold** filter has no gate and seeds on them, giving a self-consistent solution rotated by that angle — the exposure recorded under REQ-ODP-010, confined to inertial consumers, since ECEF consumers round-trip through the same table. What the TP asks for and is still owed is in the list below, now ordered by it.

**Ch. 2 of the TP on this filter, and what the measurement said (Push 73; REQ-ODP-011).** The three items are built and tested: **RTN state-noise compensation** (TP §2.2.3.1 — `accel_psd_rtn_m2_per_s3`, `Q̃ = M Q_rtn Mᵀ` at each sub-step, added to the isotropic `q_a`; Eq. 2.88 as `alongTrackPsdFromOneOrbitError` on both sides of the seam), the **DMC acceleration states** (TP §2.2.3.3 — three first-order Gauss-Markov accelerations in RTN, `dmc_tau_s`/`dmc_psd_rtn_m2_per_s5`, `∂v̇/∂a = M`, `∂ȧ/∂a = −I/τ` per Eq. 2.54, `Q = kron(Ψ, Q̃)` per Eq. 2.55 with `Ψ` by a fixed Gauss–Legendre rule because the closed forms of Eqs. 2.56–2.61 cancel catastrophically at `h/τ ~ 1e-3`; the covariance is 9×9 inside and the 6×6 pos/vel marginal outside; off at `τ = 0`), and the **covariance metrics** (TP §2.1 — `smaSigma` per Eq. 2.23, `flightPathAngleSigma` per Eq. 2.26, telemetered as `SmaSigmaM`/`FpaSigmaRad`, and the MC campaign records SMA error and σ per sample so `analysis/od` summarises the covariance on the metric that predicts). In the library the states do what the TP says: a 2e-5 m/s² along-track acceleration the model does not know is estimated to within 50 % over 900 s of 1 Hz fixes and a 300 s coast after it is closed to under half the blind error. **On the reference vehicle they are flown off, and the yaml says why with the numbers:** on `outage_horizon` (3 runs, 20 000 s) a DMC layer at τ = 600 s / σ_a = 3e-5 m/s² over the flown `q_a` *worsened* the coast (outage worst 2.35 → 3.6 m; τ = 100 s, 3.8 m) with the NEES still consistent — the 8×8 truncation on this truth is not a 600 s Gauss-Markov acceleration at 1 Hz, and the states carry noise into the coast — while `q_a`/10 alone sent the NEES to 24 against 10.5. The one candidate that improved things is the **RTN restructuring**, `q_iso` halved with `q_T` at the old `q_a`: outage worst 2.35 → 2.05 m, 95th 2.11 → 1.87 m, NEES 6.75 inside [2.74, 10.5] on 3 runs. That is a re-tuning the 30-run campaign gate had to sign off, not a 3-run sample — it did not survive it (Push 74, below) — and the MC harness now takes `--q-rtn`, `--qa-scale`, `--dmc-tau-s`, `--dmc-psd` so the study runs on the same shards. This is the TP's own caveat measured: DMC "has proved to be a best practice for applications in which there are measurements continually available to persistently excite it" — and the excitation here is the field truncation, whose spectrum is orbital harmonics, not an exponential correlation.

**What the 30-run gate said, and the criterion it broke (Push 74; REQ-ODP-012).** The RTN restructuring Push 73 measured on three runs does not survive thirty: at ten runs it is already inside the noise and at twenty it is behind — `outage_horizon` coast 95th 4.33 m against the flown 4.20 m, worst 7.24 m against 6.90 m — with the campaign NEES risen from 5.28 to 7.17, at the top of a [4.58, 7.61] interval, and the 2.35 → 2.05 m improvement that made it a candidate was a three-run sample. **The flown tuning stays**, and the reduced-`q_a` variants stay refused with numbers rather than by assertion: `q_a`/4 with the along-track term reaches NEES 11.6 and `q_a`/10 under a DMC layer 8.6, both outside the interval and both optimistic — the isotropic term is what covers the 8×8 truncation, and every way of shrinking it makes the filter claim an accuracy it does not have. The `AccelPsdRtnM2PerS3`, `DmcTauS` and `DmcPsdRtnM2PerS5` parameters stay in the filter and stay at zero on this vehicle; they exist for a vehicle whose dominant unmodelled acceleration *is* along-track and exponentially correlated, which this one's is not.

What the thirty runs did find is a **defect in the campaign's own reading**, and it is the kind §23.2 exists to catch. The flown baseline failed one criterion of twenty-four — the clean-fix rejection rate on `burn_outage_blind`, 2.2 % against a 1 % ceiling, with every other scenario at the 0.1 % the gate is configured for — and the failure was the metric, not the filter. `burn_outage_blind` flies a 120 s, 0.03 m/s² burn inside an outage with the filter told nothing: 3.6 m/s the force model never sees, leaving the state ~7.3 km from truth. When fixes return the innovation gate refuses them, and it is **right** to: the measurements are good and the state is not. The refusals are not scattered, either — they are one unbroken 898 s stretch per burn, ending the moment the coast clock reaches `MaxDegradedCoastS` from the last accepted fix, the solution is dropped, and the next fix re-seeds whole. That is the §9.2 policy working exactly as written; the filter deliberately carries no "N rejections → reseed" rule, precisely so a persistent spoof cannot win the seed early, and the price of that guarantee is paid in recovery latency.

So the rate was measuring re-acquisition latency under the gate's name, which is the same trap `analysis.od.statistics.summarise` already documents for the campaign NEES/NIS population — the regime tag falls when a fault window closes, while the estimate is still contaminated. Two changes put each number under its own name. The **clean-fix rejection rate is scoped to the samples where the filter still calls its own solution *fine***: past `MaxCoastS` the filter is telling the campaign it is coasting, and a fix refused there is not a gate false alarm. The MC driver now records the filter's own quality verdict per sample by name (`gnc::qualityName`, beside the enum for the reason `refusalName` is) rather than the analysis re-deriving it from an age and a copy of the horizon. And the latency it used to hide became a criterion of its own: the **longest unbroken stretch of delivered, un-faulted fixes the gate refuses**, bounded by the degraded horizon plus one cycle — a *stretch* and not a rate, because a rate averages a lockout over the arc and reads a filter that cannot get back for a quarter of an hour the same as one scattering refusals across a day. The bound is the filter's own configured policy, carried out of the driver in the meta record so the analysis never keeps a second copy of it, which is the flight/sim parameter-pair rule applied to a threshold. Re-run at thirty runs on records carrying both: **37 criteria, no failures** — `burn_outage_blind`'s rejection rate falls to **0.0999 %** against the 1 % ceiling, exactly the 0.1 % the gate is configured for and indistinguishable from every other scenario, while its lockout reads **899 s** against an 1801 s bound and every other scenario's is 0.02–40 s. The raw rate stays visible in the per-scenario provenance, so nothing is hidden; it is simply no longer scored as gate health. A shard written before Push 74 carries neither column and falls back to the raw rate with the new criterion skipped, rather than scoring on absent data.

The campaign itself stays a **bench artifact**, not a CI job: 30 runs × 12 scenarios × 24 h is ~3 hours on 24 cores and some gigabytes of records, and `mc-smoke` is still listed in `ci.yml` as the future stage it is. What CI does enforce is how the campaign is *read* — the criteria, the quality scoping, the lockout measurement and the pre-Push-74 fallback are unit-tested in `tests/analysis/`, so a regression in the reading fails there while the numbers themselves need the bench.

**The drag scale factor — built and measured, and it ships off (Push 76).** The §8.5 tier-3 disturbance estimation's orbit half is a dimensionless multiplier `s` on the onboard exponential-atmosphere drag term, carried as a tenth filter state — a first-order Gauss-Markov process about its nominal 1 (TP §2.2.3.4; Tapley, Schutz & Born §4.16 [tapley2004] for the augmented-state form) — with an **exact** Jacobian column, since the drag term is linear in the scale and `∂a/∂s` is that term evaluated at unit scale; the drag term was factored out of `onboardAcceleration` into `dragAcceleration` precisely so one evaluation serves as both. Its process noise reuses the DMC's discrete kernel with the state-dependent map `a_drag` in place of the constant `M_rtn`. Two policies rather than one number: the sigma is published beside the estimate (`DragScaleSigma`) because drag is observable **only** through the secular along-track signature it leaves over an arc, so on a short arc the estimate *is* the prior and the sigma is what says so; and an estimate outside `DragScaleMaxDeviation` of 1 is **refused rather than clamped** — an exponential atmosphere is not wrong by that factor, so such an estimate was driven by something that is not drag, and clamping would fly a magnitude the policy chose — with the refusal holding the last accepted scale and leaving the pos/vel solution the same fix carried untouched (`DragScaleRefused` counts them; a climbing count is a diagnosis, not a fault). Enabling it by uplink on a running filter re-seeds **that block only** to its prior, because while it was off its variance was identically zero, which is a state the Kalman gain can never reach.

**And it resolves nothing on this vehicle, which is the measurement the push is for.** `q_a` is sized from the 8×8 geopotential truncation — 1.28 m over the 300 s coast horizon, an equivalent constant 2.8e-5 m/s². The entire drag-scale signal is `(s−1)·a_drag`, and at 400 km `a_drag` is 1.2e-6 m/s², so even a **60 % density error is 7.2e-7 m/s² — 39× under the budget the filter already carries**. Measured against a truth atmosphere 60 % denser (`tests/unit/orbit_od_drag_scale_test.cpp`): after six orbits at the flown tuning the estimate is **1.023 of a true 1.6** — 4 % of the error — with σ still 0.33 of its 0.5 prior. **Arc length is not the lever** (one orbit gives 1.006, six give 1.023); `q_a` is — at `q_a`/1000 the same filter reaches 1.516 with σ 0.158 in a *single* orbit. And `q_a` cannot be reduced: Push 74 measured every reduced-`q_a` variant failing the NEES consistency gate, precisely because `q_a` covers that truncation. The two are locked, so **what unblocks the drag scale factor here is a higher-degree onboard geopotential, not a longer pass** — σ_a would have to reach the 4 cm/300 s class, a degree-20-plus field against the 8×8 flown. This is the same shape of result as the tier-3 *dipole* estimator's, which measured its own signal 40× under the tier-2 observer's noise floor: the capability ships tested, parameterised and telemetered, with the sigma that makes its own limit legible at runtime, and it is enabled on the vehicle or the onboard field whose numbers say it resolves something. REQ-ODP-013.

**Still owed.** An **SRP** scale factor — which needs an SRP *term* first, since the onboard force model carries none, and at 400 km that term is an order below the drag it would sit beside, so it is deferred with its number rather than built speculatively — and a **thrust scale factor** through a burn (TP §2.2.3.4) in place of the burn σ folded into `Q` as white; a Fraser–Potter **smoother** in `analysis/od` for BETs and the filter–smoother consistency test (Ch. 10) once there is receiver data to tune on; full-covariance telemetry as a commissioning data product (§9.3). **The correlated GNSS error and the consider block — Push 77.** Every OD number this project had measured was taken against a receiver whose position error is white, which the §6.2 model always admitted understates a real one. The receiver now carries a first-order Gauss-Markov common-mode component, expressed as a **fraction of the datasheet variance** so the total accuracy is unchanged and only the *spectrum* differs — adding a correlated term on top of the datasheet would model a worse receiver than the part described, and would confound "bigger" with "correlated" when only the second defeats the filter. The receiver reports the full total and nothing about the colour, which is the generous reading of what a formal solution covariance knows, and the filter is defeated by it anyway. On the reference vehicle the split is an engineering **allocation**, labelled as one: half the variance common-mode on a 600 s timescale, SISRE-dominated because the OEM7600 is multi-frequency.

**What it costs, and why the obvious repair is not a repair.** Against the gate's own χ² intervals, the honest receiver takes campaign NEES to 23.6 and NIS to 1.68 — **both criteria fail**. The first attempt inflated `R` by the correlated variance, the textbook remedy for unmodelled measurement error, tuned until NEES landed in its band. That fixed NEES and **destroyed NIS** (to 0.26 at the tuned value), and the reason is structural rather than a mistuning. `S = HP̄Hᵀ + R` follows from `E[e·vᵀ] = 0`, which is a *consequence* of whiteness: the prior error is a functional of past measurements, and white noise is independent of all of them.
