With a coloured error the past fixes all carried nearly the same bias (at the flown τ/Δt = 60 the neighbour correlation is 0.983), so `C = E[e·bᵀ] ≠ 0` and the true innovation covariance is `HP̄Hᵀ + R − HC − CᵀHᵀ`. Those cross terms **subtract** — the innovation is smaller than `S` by twice the bias the filter has already absorbed, measured at about **two thirds**. NEES sees that absorbed bias as state error and wants `P` larger; NIS sees it removed from the innovation and wants `S` smaller. **No single `R` satisfies both**, and the sweep says so: at inflation 0/2/3/4/6 the pair goes (23.6, 1.68) → (11.4, 0.63) → (8.07, 0.38) → (6.33, 0.26) → (4.74, 0.15), NEES entering its band only at 3 and NIS below its floor from the start.

**The fix is to carry the error, not to pad the covariance.** Three GNSS bias states are appended to the filter (13 states), carried in ECI with the local-frame anisotropy living in a `Σ_b` refreshed per fix, and `R` returns to the white part alone. That puts `C` where it belongs, as the `P_rb` block *inside* `S`: the augmented model has white noise by construction, so the innovations are white and `S` is genuinely their covariance. It ships in **consider (Schmidt) form** — the block's covariance propagates and shapes the gain, the estimate stays pinned at zero — for three independent reasons. The bias is not resolvable at the flown `q_a` (4 cm of signal over the filter's 228 s memory against a 17.7 cm floor, **the same `q_a` lockout that blocks the drag scale factor**, 4.4× under rather than 39×); consistency does not depend on resolving it, since `P_rb` and `P_bb` still reach `S`, so an unobservable bias costs the estimate and not the covariance; and a pinned estimate is a state a slow spoof cannot walk, which §9.2 leaves open and which a 600 s FOGM position bias is exactly the shape to absorb.

**Measured against a prediction made before the code existed.** Predicted NEES 6.3 ± 0.8, NIS 2.9 ± 0.30; measured **5.548 and 2.938**, both inside their bands at 32 %/35 % margin, rejection rate 0.076 %. The stated falsifier — *both statistics passing only at some `f` other than the receiver's declared value, which would mean the block was compensating rather than modelling* — did not fire: they pass at the declared `f` = 0.5, which `configc` cross-checks against the hardware entry by **plain equality**. That equality is the point of expressing the split as a fraction: the abandoned inflation factor was fitted against NEES and therefore unfalsifiable, since any value that passed was by definition right. The mechanism is confirmed directly by a τ sweep at zero inflation, where NIS returns to its white value as the error becomes white — 2.969 (white), 2.954 (τ = 1 s), 2.879 (τ = 10 s = the fix cadence), 2.408 (60 s), 1.676 (600 s) — and the control is on record: a white receiver at 30 runs measures NEES 5.283 / NIS 2.986, matching the archived pre-Push-77 campaign to three decimals. A **bias state as a full estimator** is available behind one parameter and is not flown, for the spoof reason above. REQ-ODP-014.

**Tuning got faster, which is the other half of the push.** The campaign driver gained `--gnss-corr-fraction/-tau-s/-inflate` alongside the Push 73 knobs, and the tuning loop is now documented as one scenario over a short arc — `--scenario nominal --runs 12 --duration-s 11000`, minutes rather than the full gate's hours — because NEES is measured on the nominal scenario alone. The full campaign validates the winner rather than being iterated on.

And, as before: the raw pseudorange/Doppler path and the receiver-clock states that come with it; the accelerometer as a second, measured source of the non-gravitational acceleration (bias state + burn-active gate; §8.3 above); the OD cross-check in FreeFlyer that §23.1 records as waiting on this section existing; and, of the §9.2 GNSS rows, the **slow spoof ramp** on the topology — Push 66 flew the step spoof, the stale receiver clock, `OD_RESET`, a second-receiver failover and a full orbit period as SITL rows (`tests/integration/sitl_od_fault_test.cpp`), and pinned the residual the solution-domain filter cannot close: a spoof that outlasts the **degraded** horizon (1800 s since Push 70; it was the 300 s fine horizon before) wins the re-seed, and truth is then the outlier (the raw-measurement path above is what closes it). The ramp produces no event on the topology, so its measure stays the §23.2 campaign's position error against truth.

### 8.4 Guidance
- **Pointing modes** generate reference attitude/rate: sun-point, nadir, LVLH, inertial hold, star-track, ground-track.
- **Slew planning:** eigenaxis slews with rate/accel limits; **constrained attitude guidance** honoring keep-out cones (star-tracker vs Sun/Earth/Moon) and keep-in cones (comms, solar-array sun-pointing).
- **Maneuver targeting:** compute burns to reach target Keplerian elements / altitude; **finite-burn reference generation is the default for every burn type** — impulsive solutions are first-guess/analysis approximations only, always refined to and executed as finite burns (§17).

### 8.5 Control
- **B-dot** detumble (MTQ-based), designed around the **MTQ/MAG duty-cycle interlock** (§7): field derivative from quiet-window samples only, average dipole authority scaled by the duty factor.
- **PID** for RW-based pointing.
- **RW control via L-norm / L-∞ allocation** across the pyramid (min-effort / min-max torque distribution).
- **Momentum management & desaturation:** RW momentum monitoring with MTQ (and/or thruster) desaturation.
- **CMG steering law:** singularity-robust steering (singularity-robust inverse / null-motion) when CMG-equipped.
- **Actuator abstraction:** guidance/control produce a commanded body torque; the allocation layer (RW-pyramid **or** CMG-steering) is the swappable piece, keeping upstream logic actuator-agnostic.
- **Onboard disturbance estimation → feedforward (planned, with Phase 5 control).** The flight software estimates the slowly-varying environmental torque from its own on-orbit data and feeds it forward, shrinking the unmodeled error the PID has to fight. Three tiers, cheapest first:
  1. **Model-based feedforward (free):** gravity-gradient and modeled-field `m_res×B` torques computed onboard from the §8.1 attitude, the §8.3 position, and the onboard IGRF — same formulas as §5.3, using *estimated* states. No new estimator; the §5.3 sim torques now exist (Push 43) to validate it against.
  2. **Momentum-based residual-torque observer:** total system momentum `H = Jω + h_rw` is measured (gyro + wheel tachometers); its rate minus commanded/modeled torques is the *unmodeled* external torque. A low-pass/bias filter on that residual yields a body-frame disturbance estimate for feedforward — and doubles as the §9 FDIR momentum-anomaly monitor. This is the standard industrial approach and the committed baseline.
  3. **Parameter estimation:** fit the physically-parameterized sources from long-arc data — residual dipole `m_res` (regresses against the known, rotating `B`; separable from constant-in-body biases by its `×B` signature), and drag/SRP scale factors (estimated in the OD filter from orbit decay/along-track residuals, as flight OD systems conventionally do). Estimated parameters update the feedforward model and are telemetered for trending. **The dipole half is implemented** (`gnc::DipoleEstimator`, below); the drag/SRP scale factors still wait on the §8.3 orbit filter, which is what supplies the along-track residuals they are estimated from, and are therefore still planned.

**Control — implemented (Push 54), `lib/` and flight sides.** Phase 5 opens with the three laws the vehicle needs to move: `gnc::BdotController` (`lib/gnc/bdot.{hpp,cpp}`), `gnc::AttitudePid` (`lib/gnc/attitude_pid.{hpp,cpp}`) and `gnc::RwAllocator` (`lib/gnc/rw_allocation.{hpp,cpp}`), all in the flight-safe `lib/gnc` (fixed-size Eigen, no heap, no exceptions, bounded loops, finiteness-guarded output), wrapped by one new passive F´ component.

**B-dot** is the field-strength-normalised law `m = -(k/‖B‖²)Ḃ` of Avanzini & Giulietti [avanzini2012], whose gain floor `k ≥ 2ω_o(1+sin ξ)J_min` is what the reference vehicle's `BdotGainNms` is derived from. Normalising by `‖B‖²` is what makes the decay rate independent of field strength — the un-normalised form varies its authority ~4× over a polar orbit, which is the difference between saturating at the poles and doing nothing at the equator. The derivative comes from **successive quiet-window samples and their own time tags**, never the control period: under the §7 interlock a usable sample does not arrive every cycle, and dividing by the nominal period scales the whole command by whatever the real interval was. The demand is divided by the duty factor before saturation so the *average* dipole over a period is what the gain asked for, and saturation is **per rod** — componentwise clamping preserves the sign of every term of `m·Ḃ`, so a saturated B-dot stays monotonically dissipative, which a direction-preserving scale would also be but at lower authority. (Direction preservation is the right rule for a *pointing* torque and the wrong one here; the allocation layer does the opposite, deliberately.)

**The pointing PID** is the quaternion-feedback regulator of Wie, Weiss & Arapostathis [wie1989]: `δθ = 2·sgn(δq₀)·vec(q_ref ⊗ q̂⁻¹)`, whose sign factor is what makes it take the short way round rather than unwind a 181° error the long way. The integrator is clamped *and* frozen while the torque is saturated (Åström & Murray §11.4 [astrom2008]) — the clamp alone leaves it pinned against a disturbance larger than the available torque, and it then takes as long to unwind as it took to fill. Torque saturation scales the whole vector, so an over-demand is a slower correction about the right axis rather than a faster one about the wrong axis. **Allocation** solves `A u = τ` on the wheel array, where `A` is the *negated* spin axes (a wheel's reaction on the body is `−Iω̇`, and that sign lives in exactly one place, where the config is read): L2 through a pseudo-inverse factorised once at construction, and exact **L-∞** for a four-wheel array by minimising the convex piecewise-linear `max|u_p + αn|/L` — each wheel's torque in units of its own limit `L_i`, so with unequal limits it is the box utilisation that is minimised and the array literally saturates as late as possible (Push 69; equal limits reduce it to the plain max) — over the one-dimensional null space — a bounded scalar search with `α = 0` in the candidate set, so L-∞ is never worse than L2, and pinned against an independent brute-force null-space search. Arrays of five or more wheels have a null space of dimension ≥ 2 whose exact min–max is a linear program this module does **not** solve; it refuses with `kMinMaxUnsupported` and the component falls back to L2 with one event, rather than returning an L2 answer under an L-∞ label.

**`flight.AttitudeController`** (`flight/PolarisFsw/AttitudeController/`) is the wrapper: a passive component, member **1** of the barrier-driven 10 Hz GNC rate group, running immediately after the estimator on the estimate that member just published. Modes are `IDLE`/`DETUMBLE`/`POINT`, commanded by `CTRL_MODE_SET`; the pointing reference is a commanded inertial hold (`CTRL_SET_TARGET_Q`), since guidance modes are §8.4/Phase 7. `POINT` refuses — with an event, leaving the mode where it was — unless the estimate is fresh, valid and inside `MaxAttSigmaRad`; that floor is stated as a bound on the *error being closed* rather than as a mode name, because a bound is what a pointing law actually needs. `IDLE` is accepted unconditionally, because the way out of a bad state must never itself have a precondition, and a refused cycle commands **zero** rather than saying nothing — an actuator nobody re-commands keeps driving. **Autonomous entry into DETUMBLE on rate is deliberately not here:** §10 makes that the mode manager's decision, so this push ships the *predicate* (the rate entry/exit hysteresis, telemetered as `DetumbleComplete`) and commanded transitions only. All 30 tuning values are `ParameterDb` parameters with no defaults (§19.3) — a missing one leaves the controller inert in IDLE with one `ConfigInvalid` — which took the vehicle to 85 parameters and the `PrmDb` store from 64 entries to 128. Satisfies REQ-ACTL-001, REQ-ACTL-002 and REQ-ACTL-003.

**What B-dot can honestly promise, measured.** From a 5 °/s tip-off the reference vehicle reaches 3.14 °/s 200 s **after the law engages** — which is not at boot: B-dot's only input is the voted magnetometer field, that field needs the onboard IGRF, and the IGRF needs a position, so the vehicle's 34 s GNSS cold start is 34 s in which no detumble law of any kind can act. It then decays ~1 % per 500 s. That is not a defect: a B-dot law damps only the rate components *perpendicular* to the field, and the component along it produces no `Ḃ` in body axes at all. What removes the residual is the field direction turning over the orbit — a ~1e-3 rad/s process against a ~5e-2 rad/s spin — so the vehicle settles into a slow spin about the local field line and unwinds it over **orbits**, which is exactly the asymptotic convergence [avanzini2012] proves rather than the minutes an eyeballed requirement would have claimed. REQ-ACTL-001 is therefore written on the fast phase, the one that decides controllability, and its window opens at engagement rather than at boot; the orbital-timescale tail is owed to a Monte Carlo campaign. Commanded inertial hold settles to **0.0094°** of truth-side pointing error against a 1.0° requirement (Push 60's re-sized `RW-X` wheel and its 0.8 rad/s loop; the same row measured 0.41° on the pre-Push-60 0.5 N·m·s wheel).

  The MEKF's gyro-bias states (§8.1) already absorb what a *sensor*-side bias explains; the observer targets what they cannot — true external torque. The two are distinguishable because a gyro bias does not move wheel momentum. NIS trending (§8.1) flags when the disturbance environment departs from the estimated model.

**Momentum management and disturbance feedforward — implemented (Push 56), `lib/` and flight sides.** Phase 5 closes with the half of §8.5 that decides whether a wheel-controlled vehicle can stay controlled: what the wheels are storing, how it comes off, and what torque put it there. `gnc::MomentumManager` and `gnc::mtqDesaturation` (`lib/gnc/momentum.{hpp,cpp}`) and `gnc::DisturbanceObserver` with its tier-1 model functions (`lib/gnc/disturbance.{hpp,cpp}`) join the Push 54 laws in the flight-safe `lib/gnc`, and `flight.AttitudeController` gained the wheel tachometers, fourteen parameters, one command and the two §9 monitors that go with them.

**Desaturation is the cross-product law** `m = k_d(Δh × B)/‖B‖²` (Camillo & Markley [camillo1980]; Markley & Crassidis §7.5 [markley2014]), which gives `τ = −k_d Δh_⊥` and therefore `d‖Δh‖²/dt ≤ 0`. It is field-normalised for the reason B-dot is — the *torque*, and so the unloading rate, must not vary fourfold between equator and pole — and divided by the §7 duty factor for the reason B-dot is, so the average dipole over a period is what the gain asked for. The per-rod clamp is shared with B-dot (one `clampDipoleToRods`, so the two laws cannot saturate differently) and its dissipativity argument transfers **term by term**: writing `v = B × Δh`, the law makes each `mᵢ` carry the opposite sign to `vᵢ`, `d‖Δh‖²/dt = 2 Σᵢ mᵢvᵢ`, and a componentwise clamp preserves every sign — which holds precisely because the rod triad is orthonormal, the same premise `applyParameters` already refused anything else on. What no instantaneous command can do is remove the momentum *parallel* to the field, and that ceiling is stated as a unit test rather than a comment.

**The envelope is where Push 55's analysis becomes an operational limit.** `MomentumManager` computes `h = W(I_w ω_w)` from the tachometers — refusing the whole cycle when any wheel reports no usable speed, because a missing term is not a small error in a sum but an unknown vector of that wheel's full magnitude — and gates it against a configured ceiling. That ceiling is **not** the wheels' capacity: it is the stored momentum at which the per-axis margins of REQ-ACTL-006 stop describing the vehicle (§8.5, "SISO validity boundary"), which on the reference vehicle is 1.16e-2 N·m·s at its 1.161 rad/s crossover — 24 % of the 4.9e-2 N·m·s the four-wheel pyramid guarantees in every direction. The committed `MomentumEnvelopeNms` is 7.2e-3 N·m·s, and `tests/analysis/test_control_momentum_envelope.py` **recomputes the bound from the same YAML** through `analysis.control.plant.siso_coupling` and fails if the config drifts out of the regime its own margin evidence covers. That is the closure the SISO boundary was written asking for: the analysis states a validity limit, the flight software enforces it, and a test ties the two to one file.

**The feedforward is two tiers and the anomaly monitor is one of their consumers.** Tier 1 is the modelled gravity-gradient and `m_res×B` torques on the *estimated* states — which is what put the GNSS-derived position on the `AttitudeEstimate` port, flagged valid only with both a fix and the ECEF→ECI rotation and carrying no velocity, since a velocity is what a consumer would build a propagation on and §8.3 owns that. Tier 2 differences the total system momentum `H = Jω + h_w`, subtracts tier 1, and low-passes what is left; internal torques cancel identically in `H`, so what it sees is external by construction. The estimate enters the PID demand *inside* the law, ahead of saturation and of the anti-windup decision — added afterwards it could command torque the vehicle does not have, and hidden from the integrator it would fight the integrator cancelling the same disturbance. The **same** estimate is the §9 momentum-anomaly monitor, and the observer runs whether or not its output is fed forward, because a monitor a control-tuning parameter can switch off is not a monitor.

**The anomaly budget is set by the observer's noise floor, not by the environment, and saying so is the point.** The modelled disturbances on this vehicle total ~2e-7 N·m; a 10 Hz difference quotient of `Jω` carries ~2e-4 N·m of gyro noise before filtering, and the committed 200 s low pass brings that to the low 1e-6 N·m. A threshold derived from the physics alone would therefore alarm on gyro noise every cycle. The committed `DisturbanceBudgetNm` is 2.0e-5 N·m — ~5× the filtered floor, ~100× the environment — and it names the fault class it exists for: a stuck thruster, an unlatched deployment, a residual dipole orders past its magnetic-cleanliness allocation. Both directions are flown: the SITL rows fire it on an injected 1.2e-4 N·m and prove it silent on the nominal vehicle.

**Desaturation runs concurrently with POINT and is excluded from DETUMBLE.** The wheels hold the attitude while the rods dump what the wheels are holding — two laws, one cycle, different actuators — but in DETUMBLE the rods are B-dot's, and the seam is drawn where the dipole is *formed*: exactly one law produces a body dipole in any cycle, and `commandActuators` stays the single owner of the §7 schedule that publishing one implies. Engagement is autonomous on the momentum predicate (entry/exit hysteresis with a confirmation count, so the latch provably clears), with `CTRL_DESAT` as the ground override — `FORCE` a permission rather than an instruction, so a forced cycle with no admissible field still commands nothing. Fourteen new no-default parameters take the vehicle to **100**, still inside the 128-entry `PrmDb` store (Push 59's friction feedforward adds five more, taking it to 105).

**Measured, in closed loop** (`tests/integration/sitl_attitude_control_test.cpp`, the reference vehicle against an unmodelled residual-dipole torque of ~4.5e-5 N·m). The wheels load to the committed 3.6e-3 N·m·s entry threshold, the controller engages the rods there on its own predicate, the momentum falls under the 1.08e-3 N·m·s exit threshold and the latch clears after its 5 s confirmation, settling at 6.2e-4 N·m·s. Peak stored momentum is **3.60e-3 N·m·s** — half the 7.2e-3 N·m·s envelope — under a disturbance that never stops, and worst pointing through the whole run is **0.0396°** against REQ-ACTL-002's 1.0°. The thresholds are Push 60's (the re-sized `RW-X` wheels, 0.8 rad/s loop): they moved by roughly 3.6× from the pre-Push-60 vehicle's, and the dump/reload *durations* the pre-Push-60 text quoted (~65 s to load, ~10 s to dump, an ~82 s cycle) moved with them and are deliberately not restated until the row records them.

**What the feedforward is worth, measured — and how the answer moved twice.** The same vehicle flown twice against the same injected 2.4e-4 N·m residual-dipole torque, differing only in whether the tiers are enabled, settles at **0.0409°** with feedforward and **0.0391°** without, with the §9 anomaly latching once in each run (the observer runs either way). The history is worth keeping because it is a lesson about when a comparison is measurable at all. Push 56 measured the pair at 3.45° against 3.52° — 2 %, inside the noise — and recorded that a decisive reading waited on the wheels' Coulomb friction leaving the budget, since feedforward does not address friction and friction dominated. Push 59's friction feedforward removed it and the pair separated to 1.72° against 2.10°, **18 %**: the prediction was right. Push 60's wheel and its 16× stiffer proportional gain then shrank the entire budget by a further ~40×, and at 0.04° the pair is **back inside its own noise, the feedforward run 4.6 % worse rather than better** — a difference of ~2e-4°. Nothing about the feedforward changed; what changed is that the PID alone now trims almost all of a disturbance it used to share. The SITL row's assertion is the one that survived all three readings — feedforward does not *degrade* the pointing, both figures recorded — and asserting an improvement is off the table on this vehicle rather than deferred to a later push. The monitor's evidence is the stronger half and is what the row pins: it fires on the injected torque, stays silent on the nominal vehicle, and stays silent through a full-authority detumble where B-dot's own torque is ten times the budget — because the controller subtracts the torque it commanded.

**What that row found, which is not about desaturation at all — and what an ablation then made of it (Push 59).** *All the figures in the next three paragraphs are the RW-X vehicle's, and they are kept because they are the evidence for the friction feedforward as a law; what Push 60 did to them is at the end.* Push 56 measured a *loaded* array at 2.9° against REQ-ACTL-002's 1.0°, saw the error track stored momentum, and attributed the excess arithmetically to the wheels' own Coulomb friction: RW-X carries `dry_friction_nm = 1e-4`, so once the pyramid is spinning the four friction reactions sum to `4 × 1e-4/√3 = 2.3e-4 N·m` on the body — more than twice the PID integrator's entire authority (`Ki × clamp = 1.0e-4 N·m`) — which at `Kp = 4.4e-3 N·m/rad` predicts 3.0°.

**The friction feedforward that item owed is implemented (Push 59), and the attribution was half right.** `gnc::RwFrictionCompensator` (`lib/gnc/rw_friction.{hpp,cpp}`) adds `-τ_f` per wheel *after* the §8.5 allocation and *before* the drive command, so the net rotor torque is the one the allocation asked for; the allocation is untouched, because friction is a property of a drive and folding it in would make the pseudo-inverse a function of wheel speed. The Coulomb term's `sgn(ω)` runs through a bounded linear blend over a configured deadband speed — Karnopp's zero-velocity band [karnopp1985] in feedforward form, since a bare `sign()` chatters at a wheel's zero crossing and injects a square wave of amplitude `2τ_c` at exactly the operating point where the vehicle is otherwise quietest — and **the band's cost is stated rather than hidden**: inside it the friction is deliberately under-compensated, bounded by `τ_c`, which is the error the vehicle already lived with. The compensation never exceeds the modelled friction at a trim `k ≤ 1` [armstrong1994], the torque-box clamp is applied so that what it removes is the *compensation* and never the control demand, and a wheel with no usable tachometer has no speed sign and is passed through uncompensated rather than given a guessed one. Five no-default parameters carry the model, and the coefficients are checked by the config compiler against the installed wheels' catalog entries so the flight number cannot drift from the hardware it describes.

**Then the drive was ablated term by term rather than the narrative being trusted.** Worst pointing on the loaded row, same orbit and seed: 2.77° uncompensated; **1.38°** with the feedforward as flown; 1.73° with the plant's Coulomb friction deleted outright and no feedforward; 1.85° with the drive's torque quantization deleted instead; 0.18° with neither non-ideality. So friction was **about half** of the 2.9°, and the other half is the **drive torque LSB** — RW-X quotes `torque_quantization_nm = 1e-4`, the same size as the Coulomb friction, so a per-wheel demand under half an LSB is commanded as zero and the loop carries a ±5e-5 N·m dead zone. That is a second drive-level item (dither, a speed-mode inner loop, or a finer drive), recorded as owed — and Push 60 closed it without building any of them, by flying a wheel whose LSB is 8e-6 N·m rather than 1e-4 (below).

**The trim is a closed-loop parameter, not a safety margin — and this is the part the open-loop argument misses.** "Smaller residual disturbance" is not "better pointing": the friction being cancelled was also passively *damping* the stored wheel momentum, and a near-empty array runs at the speeds where the compensation's sign is least trustworthy. Flown across both operating points, worst pointing in degrees goes 2.77 / 0.12 uncompensated, to **1.38 / 0.25** at `k = 0.5`, to 1.52 / **1.09** at `k = 1.0` — loaded array first, near-empty second. Full compensation is worse than half on *both*, and at `k = 1` it breaks REQ-ACTL-002's own verification row. The reference vehicle therefore flies `WheelFrictionScale = 0.5`, and the policy that the trim stays at or below 1 is documented rather than clamped in flight, because silently rewriting a commanded trim would hide the one case an operator needs to see.

One consequence worth naming: with the friction paid for directly, the old coupling in which pointing error tracked stored momentum is gone, so Push 56's claim that every desaturation window left the pointing better than it found it is no longer physics. The SITL row's assertion moved with it — the worst pointing while a rod is energised (0.92°) was compared against the worst while none is (1.38°), which is what the concurrency feature actually owns.

**And in Push 60 that comparison had to change shape again, for the opposite reason.** The re-sized wheel carries 8e-6 N·m of Coulomb friction and an 8e-6 N·m drive LSB — an order of magnitude under the old part on both terms the ablation named — so on the current vehicle the quiet-phase error collapses to **0.0203°** and the worst during a desaturation window is **0.0396°**. The rods are now the *dominant* term inside their own windows and the ratio has risen to ~2, while the absolute error is 25× inside REQ-ACTL-002. A ratio against a near-zero baseline is evidence that everything else got quiet, not evidence of coupling, so the row bounds the rod contribution **absolutely** (< 0.05°, where the requirement is written) and keeps a loose 5× band as a sanity check. The relative claim is retired rather than restored: it was a meaningful inequality only while something larger than the rods was setting the baseline.

*Reference: Camillo & Markley 1980 [camillo1980] (unloading law), Wie §7 [wie2008] (system momentum with wheels), Hughes §5.2 [hughes1986] and Wertz §17.2 [wertz1978] (gravity gradient), Armstrong-Hélouvry et al. 1994 [armstrong1994] (friction models and model-based compensation) and Karnopp 1985 [karnopp1985] (the zero-velocity band). Satisfies REQ-ACTL-009, REQ-ACTL-010 and REQ-ACTL-011.*

**Tier 3, the dipole half — implemented, `lib/gnc/dipole_estimation.{hpp,cpp}`.** Tier 1 feeds forward `m_res × B` using `residual_dipole_am2` from config, which is a magnetic-**cleanliness allocation** — the largest moment the build is allowed to have, not a measurement of the moment it has. `gnc::DipoleEstimator` replaces the allocation with a fit against flight data, using the tier-2 observer's unmodelled-torque estimate as its measurement: `τ = m × B = −[B×]m` is linear in the unknown, so each accepted cycle is a three-row linear measurement and the estimator is information-form recursive least squares (Inamori, Sako & Nakasuka [inamori2011] for the on-orbit identification; Ljung §11.2 [ljung1999] for the recursion and its excitation condition), an O(1) 3×3 accumulator on a flight path. The **drag scale factor** is the orbit-side half and shipped with Push 76 (§8.3), where it measured the same verdict this one did on this vehicle — the signal 39× under the process noise that covers the geopotential truncation — and ships disabled for the same honest reason. The SRP scale factor stays owed, and needs an onboard SRP term before it can have a coefficient.

**`−[B×]` is rank 2 at every instant, so observability is tested rather than assumed.** The component of `m` along `B` is invisible at any single epoch — the same structural fact that makes the rods instantaneously rank 4 of 6 (REQ-ACTL-007) — and the three components separate only as the field direction turns over the orbit. Publishing from a rank-deficient information matrix would fabricate the third component out of the pseudo-inverse's null space, so two **different** gates run and the refusal names which one closed, because "keep tumbling" and "keep collecting" are different instructions to the ground. The first is `λ_min/λ_max` of the accumulated information matrix — **the same ratio the flight Davenport seed gate (`SeedMinObservability`) and `analysis/control/observability.py` use**, so tier 3 and the rest of the vehicle measure observability with one function and cannot drift apart. The second is `λ_min` itself, which because the fit is preconditioned by a nominal field magnitude reads as an effective sample count on the worst-determined axis and is equivalently a bound on the published sigma. Geometry alone costs **2800 s — half an orbit — before any fit is possible**, whatever the dipole is.

**Precision saturates at the forgetting horizon, not at the pass length.** The physical moment moves with the power state, so the accumulator forgets exponentially in *time*, `w = exp(−Δt/T_f)`. The information matrix therefore reaches a steady state at ≈ `T_f/Δt` effective samples and **a longer pass buys nothing**: the sigma floor is a function of `T_f` alone — measured at 3.1e-2 A·m² for `T_f` = 3 orbits, unchanged over a pass four times longer, and 9.7e-3 A·m² at 30 orbits. The ground's only lever on precision is the memory time, traded directly against drift tracking, which is why a *commanded calibration pass* — during which the power state is held — should run a longer `T_f` than the drift-tracking default. The sample cadence is part of the estimator for the same "do not lie about your own evidence" reason: the tier-2 estimate is a 200 s low pass, so 10 Hz reads of it are the same filtered value seen 2000 times, and accumulating them would inflate the information matrix three orders of magnitude and publish a sigma three orders too small. Samples arriving inside twice the observer's time constant are rejected.

**What this can and cannot do on the reference vehicle, measured** (`tests/unit/dipole_estimation_test.cpp`). The reference allocation `[0.002, −0.001, 0.0015]` A·m² is an ~8e-8 N·m signature against the observer's 3.2e-6 N·m filtered floor — an SNR of **0.025 per sample** — and after a full day the published sigma is 1.4e-2 to 2.1e-2 A·m², an order of magnitude larger than the dipole itself. **Tier 3 cannot refine the feedforward on a magnetically clean vehicle, and the honest outcome is that the sigma says so** rather than the estimator publishing a fit it does not have. What it does resolve is a dipole of the size the §9 anomaly monitor exists for: `DisturbanceBudgetNm` = 2.0e-5 N·m is ≈ 0.7 A·m² at LEO field strength, and such a dipole is pinned to 10 % on all three axes in **5200 s — 1.4 h, under one orbit** — at `T_f` = 30 orbits. That is the calibration-pass length to give the ground. So tier 3's role here is **diagnosis**: when `MomentumAnomaly` latches, this is what names the offending moment in body axes with a sigma. Feedforward refinement is what the same code does on a vehicle whose dipole is large enough to be worth feeding forward, and the gates are what keep that distinction from being a matter of opinion rather than of measurement.

**Two limits stated rather than papered over.** First, the observer's estimate is *every* unmodelled torque — aero CP–CM, SRP, wheel friction, an unlatched deployment — and this fit assigns to `m` whatever part of them correlates with `B`. That is a **bias**, and no least-squares covariance sees a bias: the test that pins it shows the error unchanged when the memory is lengthened tenfold while the sigma falls by √10. Gating accumulation on "regimes where the dipole dominates" is deliberately *not* the answer, because there is no onboard test for that — knowing the aero and SRP torques well enough to declare them subdominant would require the orbit-side tier-3 fit that does not exist yet, and a gate built on the tier-1 model would only be checking the model against itself. Second, and consequently, what protects the vehicle *when the estimate is eventually consumed* is a check against an **independently established** fact: a wrong fit fed to tier-1 is an injected disturbance, and `residual_dipole_am2` is a cleanliness bound established on the ground. A fit outside it indicts the fit, not the vehicle, and is **refused rather than clamped** — clamping would publish a direction the data never supported at a magnitude the policy chose. Nothing outside the bound is ever published, on any cycle, and a refusal holds the last accepted estimate rather than zeroing it.

**What ships in Push 59: the estimator, not the wiring.** It lives in `lib/gnc` with its unit suite and this section; no F´ component consumes it, and no parameters reach `PrmDb`. That is a decision the measurement above made for us. Feeding it to tier-1 on this vehicle would replace a ground-established cleanliness allocation with a fit an order of magnitude noisier, and wiring it as a diagnostic would spend ten no-default parameters (the vehicle's 100 → 110) on a path that, on a clean vehicle, is designed never to fire. The estimator is therefore held ready rather than installed: the work that remains is the component seam, its ten parameters and a closed-loop row against `sim/world/magnetic_field`, and the trigger to do it is a vehicle or an investigation whose dipole is in the class the gates say is resolvable — an anomaly, a dirtier build, or a `MomentumAnomaly` latch someone needs named.

*Reference: Inamori, Sako & Nakasuka 2011 [inamori2011] (on-orbit residual-moment identification), Ljung §11.2 [ljung1999] (information-form RLS with forgetting), Markley & Crassidis §3.2.4 and §7.5 [markley2014], Wertz §17.2 [wertz1978]. Supports REQ-ACTL-011.*
- **Wheel-speed keep-out zones + on-orbit jitter characterization (planned, with §7 jitter model).** RW jitter tones (tens–hundreds of Hz) sit far above the GNC loop bandwidth, so the FSW's role is **operational avoidance, not active cancellation**: (1) momentum management enforces **wheel-speed keep-out zones** (uploaded parameter table) using the wheel array's null space to bias individual wheel speeds away from resonance crossings and zero crossings at zero net body torque; (2) an SDO-style **on-orbit jitter-characterization mode** — commanded slow wheel spin sweeps while recording high-rate IMU data as a data product — lets the ground build waterfall plots from flight data and refine the keep-out table for the as-built observatory (ground-loop estimation; the tones are unobservable to the 10 Hz onboard filter and need no onboard estimator).

**Linear analysis of the as-flown loop — implemented (Push 55), `analysis/control/`.** The margins the pointing loop actually holds are computed rather than asserted, by a Python package that reads the **same committed `config/spacecraft/*.yaml`** the FSW is tuned from and rebuilds the shipped control law as a transfer function. It is the documented exception to the "no GNC math in Python" rule (`analysis/CLAUDE.md`): linear-systems analysis on matrices from config duplicates no flight computation, and the moment it needed a quaternion or a propagation it would need `bindings/` instead. Four things it establishes, each a requirement:

- **Stability margins (REQ-ACTL-006).** Per-axis open loop `L(s) = C(s)P(s)` with `P = 1/(J_ii s²)` and `C` the shipped PID — including that the derivative term acts on the *measured* rate, so there is no derivative filter pole to model. Analysed as a **sampled-data** loop at the 0.1 s control period (ZOH equivalents of the angle and rate paths plus the flight code's forward-Euler integrator, assembled in state space because the three paths share the plant's `(z−1)` factors and a transfer-function sum leaves an uncancelled pole at `z = 1`). The reference vehicle holds **60.1°/60.1°/63.1°** of phase margin and **25.5/25.5/23.9 dB** of gain margin against a 30°/6 dB requirement, with a sensitivity peak of 1.06–1.07 and disk margins of 10.1–11.0 dB / 55–59° (Push 60 tuning: `ω_n` = 0.8 rad/s, crossovers 1.161 and 1.361 rad/s).
- **The gain margin of this loop is two-sided, and saying so is the point.** An integrator on a double integrator is **type 3**: the phase starts at −270° and rises through −180° at `√(Ki/Kd)` while `|L| > 1`, so the loop is conditionally stable and a standard margin call returns a *negative* number — −27 dB on this perfectly healthy design. The toolkit reports both directions and the requirement is written on the smaller, which on the Push 60 tuning is the upward one (25.5 dB up against 27.1 dB down on X/Y; 23.9 against 28.6 on Z). On the pre-Push-60 tuning the downward direction bound at 15.0 dB; which side binds is a property of a tuning, not of the loop's structure, which is why the requirement names the smaller rather than a side.
- **Controllability (REQ-ACTL-007).** Kalman rank plus finite-horizon Gramians (the infinite-horizon one does not exist — the double integrator is not Hurwitz) for the pyramid, all four 3-of-4 failure subsets, and the rods. The subsets come out at a conditioning of 0.250 against the flight allocator's own `AllocMinConditioning` gate of 0.05, which is the comparison that makes "controllable" mean "the vehicle would actually allocate on it". The magnetorquers are **instantaneously rank 4 of 6** — `m×B` has no component along `B̂` — and reach rank 6 only averaged over an orbit of the tilted-dipole field, at 1.5× the wheels' Gramian condition number. That is the same fact REQ-ACTL-001's honest 200 s bound is written on, arrived at from the linear algebra instead of from a simulation.
- **Observability (REQ-ACTL-008).** The (attitude error, gyro bias) model under two vector measurements, measured with **the same information-matrix ratio the flight `SeedMinObservability` gate uses** — reproduced against the config's own closed form to 1e-9, so the analysis and the gate cannot drift. 0.177 at 90° separation, 4.2e-3 at the committed `MinSinAngle` gate (sin θ = 0.17, i.e. 9.8°), and rank 4 of 6 in eclipse, where rotation about the field line and the bias along it are unobservable by construction.

**No control-systems library, and why that is the cheaper choice.** A loop here is a pair of polynomials plus a sample period, evaluated with `numpy.polyval` and discretised with `scipy.signal.cont2discrete`; the margin extraction, the disk margin and the Gramians are Polaris's own. Everything the analysis needs is polynomial arithmetic and root finding, and owning ~200 lines of it costs less than a dependency whose margin conventions would then have to be re-derived anyway — as the conditional-stability finding above shows, the library answer for this loop is a number the requirement cannot be written on. What owning it *does* cost is the obligation to prove it, so the margin extractor is validated against closed-form cases before it is pointed at the vehicle: the third-order textbook loop `1/[s(s+1)(s+2)]` whose gain margin is exactly 6 (15.563 dB) at ω = √2 [ogata2010], a PD-controlled double integrator with a closed-form crossover and phase margin, the disk margin's exact value on a constant-gain loop, and the vehicle's own continuous loop against its hand-derived polynomial.

**Every analysis produces a report and plots that carry the verdict (standing convention, user decision 2026-08-04).** `analysis/common/` holds the shared pieces so this is a pattern rather than one push's habit: a `Criterion`/`AnalysisReport` pair carrying requirement ID, threshold, measured value, margin in absolute and percentage terms, verdict, the configuration provenance and the modelling assumptions in force; a plain-text renderer written beside the figures; and threshold-annotation plotting helpers. The figures state their verdict on their face — the GM/PM requirement bounds drawn on the Bode axes with the measured margins annotated at the frequencies they were measured at, the disk-margin exclusion disc drawn on the Nyquist plot, the wheel-failure conditioning as bars against the flight allocator's own gate — with the value and the word PASS or FAIL in text, never colour alone. Tests assert on the **structured** report; plot generation is a smoke test. Figures and the rendered report go to a caller-supplied directory under `build-artifacts/` and are not committed. The momentum-budget, detumble-MC, contact-scheduling and link-budget tools reuse `analysis/common/` rather than reinventing it.

**SISO validity boundary and MIMO roadmap (committed, user decision 2026-08-04).** The Push 55 toolkit is deliberately **per-axis SISO**, and that is valid only while the plant is near-diagonal: a three-axis-stabilised rigid body, inertia near principal axes, and stored wheel momentum small enough that the gyroscopic coupling `ω×(Jω + h_w)` is negligible against the control torque at the loop bandwidth. Both conditions are *checked*, not assumed — `load_vehicle` refuses a config with products of inertia, and the margin report computes the coupling ratio `ρ = ‖h_w‖/(J_min·ω_c)` at the measured crossover against a 0.1 limit. On the reference vehicle that check **fires**: with the four-wheel array at its 0.030 N·m·s-per-wheel capacity, ρ is 0.60 (X/Y) and 0.51 (Z) against the 0.1 limit, so the shipped margins hold only for stored momentum below **1.16e-2 N·m·s** — 24 % of the 4.9e-2 N·m·s the pyramid guarantees in every direction. That is the Push 60 vehicle; the same check on the pre-Push-60 tuning gave 2.7e-3 N·m·s, under 1 % of one RW-X wheel, and what closed most of that gap is the bandwidth (0.197 → 0.8 rad/s) rather than the smaller wheel. The bound scales as `J_min · ω_c`, so it is the *crossover* and not the inertia that sets it: growing the vehicle moves the bound and the momentum it must cover by the same factor and changes nothing — which is exactly why Push 60's repair was a smaller wheel and a faster loop rather than a bigger bus. That is not a defect in the margins; it is the honest statement of what a per-axis analysis covers, and it is the reason the following is a committed roadmap item rather than a footnote. Each of these triggers requires **multivariable analysis** — multivariable loop shaping, disk margins with all channels perturbed simultaneously at the plant input *and* output, and structured-singular-value (μ) robustness where the uncertainty structure warrants it:

- **CMGs (§7, §18.9).** A steering law plus gimbal dynamics couples all three axes by construction. MIMO with no asterisk.
- **Flexible appendages (§5.4).** Array and boom modes in or near the control bandwidth rarely align with a single control axis, so control–structure interaction analysis is multivariable from the start.
- **Momentum-biased operation / large stored wheel momentum.** `h_w×ω` makes roll and yaw a coupled nutation system; per-axis Bode plots describe a vehicle that does not exist. This is the trigger the reference vehicle already trips at capacity, and the one **Push 56's momentum management makes operationally real**.
- **LQR (and any future MPC).** A full-state Riccati gain `K` is a full matrix, so a rigorous robustness statement for it *is* a MIMO disk-margin computation. Loop-at-a-time is the first check, never the last.

The implementation choice is **deliberately deferred to the push that needs it**: extending the native scipy modules to MIMO frequency response and singular-value plots is the cheaper path if all that is wanted is multivariable disk margins, while μ-analysis realistically means adopting `python-control` plus `slycot`. Choosing now would be choosing without the requirement that decides it.

**Alternate control laws (committed roadmap).** The `AttitudeController` interface — a mode enum, a parameter set, and law functions in `lib/gnc` — is the extension seam for both of the following. Neither is a component rework: a new law is a parameter plus a `lib/gnc` function.

- **LQR — planned (Phase 11 timeframe, or sooner if per-vehicle PID tuning across the hardware catalog gets expensive).** The gain is computed **on the ground** from the linearised attitude-plus-wheel dynamics on the vehicle's actual inertia tensor — a Riccati solve, which is exactly the kind of iterative numerics the §3.6 flight standard forbids onboard — and flies as a fixed-size matrix multiply `u = −K·[δθ; δω]`, which it does not. The `Q`/`R` weights are where the pointing-error-versus-wheel-effort trade is made explicit, instead of being implicit in three hand-placed gains; integral action comes either from state augmentation or from reusing the existing conditional integrator. What this buys over the shipped PID is a principled multi-axis trade on a vehicle whose inertia is not diagonal, which the reference 6U's diagonal `J` does not need — hence the timing.
- **MPC — considered and deferred, deliberately.** Model-predictive control needs an onboard QP solver: iterative, with an iteration count that depends on the data, and heap-hungry in every off-the-shelf implementation — against a flight standard that forbids exactly those three things. That cost would have to buy something, and on this vehicle it does not: the binding constraints are **box constraints on four wheels**, which the exact L-∞ allocator already solves in closed form and to optimality; keep-out-cone slews are a *guidance*-layer problem (§8.4 plans the trajectory, the tracking controller follows it), not a control-layer one. The revisit criterion is a mission with genuinely **coupled** constraints — agile imaging holding momentum, cone and jitter limits simultaneously, where the constraint set does not factor into an allocation and a path plan. Until then MPC would be a solver in the flight image earning nothing. This is a decision, not a TODO.


**FINDING AND RESOLUTION (Push 60): the committed wheels and the committed torque rods were not sized for the same spacecraft, and the rods are what pins the vehicle.** `analysis/sizing` measured the four RW-X wheels at **78×** the largest momentum any sizing driver asks of the 12 kg bus — one wheel could spin the whole vehicle to 239 °/s, and the vehicle desaturated at ~12 rpm of a 6000 rpm rotor. The obvious repair is to grow the spacecraft to suit the wheel, and it was tried: 50 kg / `J = diag(4.71, 4.71, 4.08)` takes the oversizing to 2.0× and every sizing criterion to PASS. **Magnetic detumble does not survive that move**, and the reason is Avanzini & Giulietti's own convergence condition read at the *saturated* gain. The floor `k ≥ 2ω_o(1 + sin ξ)J_min` [avanzini2012] is stated for the unsaturated law; B-dot rails its rods for the whole detumble, so its effective gain is `m_sat‖B‖/ω`, which falls as the vehicle spins faster, and the law converges only below

> `ω_max = η·m·‖B‖_min·duty / (2ω_o(1 + sin ξ)·J_min)`

which is **15.3 °/s** at `J_min = 0.10 kg·m²` — the 5 °/s tip-off is comfortably inside it — and **0.37 °/s** at `J_min = 4.08`, below even the 1.71 °/s the magnetometer can resolve. The 50 kg bus has no convergent B-dot operating point at all with the catalog's 15 A·m² rods, and `SitlAttitudeControl.DetumblesFromFiveDegreesPerSecond` measured exactly that: the rate fell from 5 °/s to 4.50 °/s in ~800 s with all three rods railed and then stopped, flat to five figures out to 4000 s. Two A/B runs isolated it — the same flight tuning on a `diag(0.12, 0.12, 0.10)` plant passes, and the 50 kg plant with the rods temporarily raised to 200 A·m² passes, the 13.3× the expression predicts. The same shortfall showed up in three more §8.5 mechanisms on that bus (desaturation entry had no value that was both reachable by a slew and dumpable by the rods; the §9 anomaly budget, which scales with `J` because the observer differences `Jω + h`, outran the rods' unloading authority 6×; and B-dot's residual handed POINT essentially the whole certified envelope), all of them the same ratio.

**So the rods fix the vehicle at the small end, and the wheel was the mis-sized part.** The resolution is a **re-sized wheel rather than a re-baselined vehicle**: the catalog's generic entry (`config/hardware/reaction_wheel/rwx.yaml`) becomes 0.030 N·m·s / 0.002 N·m with 8e-6 N·m friction and an 8e-6 N·m drive LSB — the CubeSpace CW0.03 / Sinclair RW-0.03 class — which sits inside the [8.3, 64] mN·m·s window the sizing tool derives for this bus — above the 5 °/s tip-off with 30 % margin, below the 10× oversizing gate. The 12 kg mass and `J = diag(0.12, 0.12, 0.10)` are unchanged. The loop was retuned around the new part (`ω_n` 0.197 → 0.8 rad/s at `ζ` = 0.7; `PidMaxTorqueNm` 0.02 → 0.0015; the momentum envelope 2.0e-3 → 7.2e-3 N·m·s), and `analysis/sizing` now reports **PASS with zero failing criteria** at an oversizing factor of 4.7×. Two things fell out that no tuning pass had been able to buy: `DetumbleExitRadps` now sits **above** B-dot's 1.71 °/s noise floor (1.9 °/s committed, against 1.72 °/s from the momentum bound — two bounds that were mutually unsatisfiable on the old vehicle now agree within 1 %), and the drive-quantization term Push 59 recorded as the barrier to REQ-ACTL-002 fell with the LSB, taking the loaded array from 1.38° to **0.0396°**.

**What the sizing tool missed, and the criterion that closes it (Push 61).** Its M2 criterion is an *impulse* bound (rod torque × time budget) and passed the 50 kg vehicle with 25 % margin; it says in its own note that the residual spin about the field line is outside its scope. The saturated-gain condition above is now **M4** in `analysis/sizing/magnetorquers.py`: `ω_max = η·m·‖B‖_min·duty / (2ω_o(1+sin ξ)·J_min)`, judged against the tip-off at the same 30 % margin as every other row. It reports **15.29 °/s** on the committed 12 kg bus against a 5 °/s tip-off, and **0.37 °/s** on the 50 kg candidate — which is the point: that vehicle passes M2 with +26 % and fails M4, so the tool now refuses in seconds the design that previously cost a full SITL investigation to disqualify. It binds on `J_min` rather than `J_max` (the easiest axis is the one that decides) and is a statement about the *rods against the body*, which is why a vehicle can pass every wheel criterion and still fail it.

**The two exits not taken, recorded because they are the real trade.** (1) Keep a 50 kg vehicle and re-size the rods to ~200 A·m² each (260 with the tool's 30 % margin) — coherent, since a bus flying 150 kg-class wheels would fly big-satellite rods, but it is new hardware on both ends. (2) Detumble on the wheels rather than the rods; the array can absorb the raw tip-off, but this needs a wheel detumble law that does not exist and revises the §10.1 safe-mode hierarchy, which is on record as a user decision. Both remain available for a vehicle that genuinely wants to be larger; neither is needed by the reference bus.

---

## 9. Sensor/Actuator Health: Flags & FDIR

### 9.1 Validity Flags
Per-sensor validity flag with documented criteria: range checks, rate-of-change checks, staleness/timeout, cross-sensor consistency, solution-quality flags. Downstream consumers must respect flags — invalid measurements are excluded, never silently used.

### 9.2 FDIR Architecture
- **Monitors** (per sensor/actuator and system-level) → **isolation** → **response** → **safing escalation**.
- Sensor FDIR: dropout/disagreement detection, voting where redundancy exists; **measured-vs-modeled magnetic field consistency** (onboard IGRF) as a magnetometer reasonableness check.
- **GNSS FDIR:** outage detection and graceful coasting on propagation; **spoofing/meaconing** detection via innovation/consistency checks and position/time reasonableness bounds, with measurement rejection. The truth model (`sim/sensors/gnss`) drives these with three fault sources: a commanded **outage** (loss of fix), a **spoofed** position offset that stays *valid* so it must be caught on innovation rather than a flag, and **geographic jamming** — a config-provided KML of jammed regions (`sim/sensors/gnss_jamming`, e.g. `config/scenarios/jamming/*.kml`) that invalidates the fix whenever the sub-satellite point is inside one, with the reacquisition delay applied on exit. Jamming and outage share one recovery path; the receiver telemeters *which* zone jammed it. All of this is **scenario-controlled** (distinct from the hardware catalog, which says what the receiver *is*): the `environment` block carries `gnss_jamming_enabled`, a `gnss_noise_enabled` master switch (false flies a truth-perfect receiver for bring-up), and a `gnss_fault_events` schedule of time-windowed outage/spoof/clock-jump events per unit. The schedule is resolved and applied to the receiver each step by `sim/scenario/gnss_faults` (`applyGnssFaults` reconciles the state so a window's end clears its fault) — bound by the §2.4 macro-step loop, as with the other per-step sensor sampling.
- **Estimator fallback:** loss/occlusion of star trackers triggers fine→coarse attitude estimation (§8.1); the active estimation mode is part of FDIR state.
- Actuator FDIR: wheel stall/runaway, saturation, thruster stuck-on/-off, dipole saturation.
- **Momentum envelope and momentum anomaly (implemented, Push 56).** Two monitors on the §8.5 momentum chain, both in `flight.AttitudeController` and both edge-gated. `MomentumEnvelopeExceeded` fires when the stored wheel momentum leaves the range the pointing loop's margins are *analysed* over (§8.5, "SISO validity boundary") — not a wheel-capacity alarm, and on the reference vehicle a far smaller number than one: it says the vehicle is outside the regime REQ-ACTL-006 certifies, while every wheel is still comfortable. `MomentumAnomaly` fires on the §8.5 tier-2 observer's estimate of the **unmodelled** secular external torque exceeding the configured budget for a confirmation count, and clears below a configured clear threshold (`DisturbanceClearNm`, at or under the budget) held for the same count — the latch holds its state between the two thresholds, so an estimate parked at the budget, which is what a real fault at the margin looks like through the observer's low-pass, does not cycle the event once per confirmation count. The two consumers of that one estimator are deliberate: the control layer feeds it forward, FDIR gates it, and a second estimator for the monitor would be two answers to one question. The monitor does **not** fire on the modelled environment — the gravity-gradient torque an Earth-pointing vehicle sees at twice the orbital frequency is subtracted as tier-1 feedforward before the filter, which is what stops a healthy vehicle alarming twice an orbit. Neither monitor takes an autonomous action in this push: the response is the Phase-7 state machine's (§10.1), and the honest action here is to name the condition. A wheel reporting no usable speed **refuses** the momentum computation rather than understating it, and both latches are held rather than cleared across such a cycle — absence of evidence is not evidence of recovery. The refusal is itself FDIR-visible: one dead tachometer blinds desaturation and both momentum monitors at once, so it raises `MomentumUnavailable` (naming the reason and the wheel, at the bounded alert cadence) and drops the `MomentumValid` channel, rather than leaving the silence of the monitors it disabled unattributed.
- Subsystem FDIR: low SoC (power) and thermal-limit monitors can trigger Safe mode.
- **No exceptions:** faults are F´ events with severity; every alert maps to either an autonomous FDIR action (state-machine transition / reconfiguration) or an operator alarm with a documented (possibly null) action. Responses are tiered and escalate to **Safe Mode**. FDIR state and triggers are fully telemetered.
- **Verification:** every monitor/response is exercised by the FDIR fault-injection integration suite (§23.1.1).

---

## 10. Mission Modes / State Machine

Mode manager implementing a documented state machine with explicit entry/exit conditions and transition guards. Each mode binds a guidance reference + control/gain set + active actuator/sensor configuration.

| Mode | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **Safe** | **the lowest safe-mode state = coarse sun pointing**: SS + MAG + IMU coarse estimation (§8.1) driving array-to-sun attitude — power-positive, thermally safe, star-tracker- and table-independent; FDIR sink |
| **Detumble** | B-dot rate reduction (MTQ). **Not** the resting safe state: entered from/instead of Safe only when body rate or stored momentum exceeds the controllability threshold (coarse sun pointing cannot converge) or when explicitly commanded; exits back to Safe once rates are damped |
| **Sun Point** | array-to-sun acquisition/hold |
| **Nadir Track** | Earth-pointing |
| **Star Track** | inertial target / observation pointing |
| **LVLH Pointing** | orbit-relative attitude |
| **Inertial Hold** | fixed inertial attitude |
| **Ground-Station Tracking** | track a station from the onboard GS list during contact windows |
| **Delta-V / Maneuver** | attitude + thruster sequencing for orbit-change burns (§17) |

- Transitions: autonomous (FDIR/conditions) + commanded; guards prevent illegal/unsafe transitions.
- **Onboard ground-station list** (configurable): lat/lon/alt, mask angles; used for contact prediction, tracking, and link analysis.

### 10.1 How the State Machine Is Defined, Compiled and Reviewed

*Architecture decision, user decision 2026-08-03. Design only — nothing in this
subsection is implemented; it lands in Phase 7 (§24).*

The mode table above is a specification written in prose, and the guards that
enforce it do not exist yet. The question this subsection settles is **what form
they take when they do**, because the answer decides three things that are hard
to change later: whether an operator can read the vehicle's behaviour without
reading C++, whether a SITL case can exercise a mode path without a rebuild, and
whether the §9 fault responses are *data* the fault matrix can enumerate or
*code* it can only sample.

#### 10.1.1 The declarative definition

The satellite-wide modes of §10 and the GNC submodes underneath them (the §8.2
estimator ladder, the §8.5 control-law selection, the §7 actuator
configuration) are defined in **one declarative file per vehicle**, in
`config/`, alongside every other vehicle-specific decision (§19.3). It carries:

- **states**, hierarchically — a satellite-wide mode contains submodes, and a
  submode's transitions are scoped to its parent, so "any pointing mode → Safe"
  is one transition and not eight;
- **transitions**, each naming a source state, a target state and a trigger;
- **triggers**, of exactly three kinds: a **command** opcode, an **event** (an
  FDIR alert raised by a monitor), and a **predicate becoming true** over
  telemetry;
- **guards** — boolean expressions over a **bounded predicate vocabulary**:
  comparisons on telemetry channels, enumerated-state equality, event-presence
  within a window, and a persistence count. Not a general expression language:
  the vocabulary is fixed at compile time and every term is a channel the
  vehicle already telemeters, which is what makes a guard readable from the
  ground and evaluable in bounded time;
- **entry and exit actions**, each a named, pre-registered command or port
  invocation — a closed set the executor can dispatch, never arbitrary code.

The config compiler (`tools/configc`, §19.3) validates it — every state
reachable, every target defined, no transition whose guard references a channel
the dictionary does not carry, exactly one initial state, and a Safe sink
reachable from every state — and emits **flight tables** into `ParameterDb`
alongside the rest of the tuning. There are no defaults: a vehicle whose table
is missing or fails validation refuses to leave its initial state and says so,
the same rule §19.3 applies to every other parameter.

#### 10.1.2 The executor

One generic F´ component consumes the tables: a **bounded table interpreter**,
not generated logic. It holds the current state, evaluates the guards of the
outgoing transitions of the active state and its ancestors in table order on its
rate-group tick, dispatches entry/exit actions, and telemeters the state, the
last transition, and the guard that fired. Flight rules apply in full and are
what bound it: fixed-size tables sized at compile time, no heap, no recursion
(hierarchy is walked as a bounded loop up a parent-index array), an explicit cap
on transitions evaluated per tick, and every dispatch return code checked.

This is the standard table-driven mode manager of flight practice rather than an
invention — the pattern is old enough to be unremarkable, and its value here is
the same as everywhere: the machine is a *reviewable artifact* instead of a
control-flow graph distributed across handlers.

#### 10.1.3 The alternative considered: F´ native FPP state machines

F´ **v4.2.2** — the version vendored under `fprime/` — has real state-machine
support in FPP, and it is better than a first look suggests. `fpp-to-cpp`
generates a `<M>StateMachineBase` class from a `state machine` definition, and
the language covers **hierarchical states with nested initial transitions,
entry/exit actions, guards, choice pseudo-states, and signals with typed
payloads** (see `fprime/docs/user-manual/framework/state-machines.md` and the
worked models in `fprime/FppTestProject/FppTest/state_machine/`). It also
supports *external* machines produced by JPL's STARS autocoder from a UML/Quantum
Modeler model, which is the route several JPL missions actually fly. None of
that is a toy, and adopting it would cost no new flight machinery at all.

Two properties decide against it as the **satellite-wide** mode manager:

1. **Guards and actions are hand-written C++.** FPP generates a pure-virtual
   `guard_<g>` and `action_<a>` per guard and action; the component implements
   them. So the states and transitions become data, and the *conditions* — which
   are exactly what §9 FDIR reasons about and what the fault matrix has to
   enumerate — stay code. A machine whose skeleton is declarative and whose
   guards are not is not reviewable as a diagram, because the diagram cannot say
   what `g` means.
2. **The machine is not in the dictionary.** `fpp-to-dict` emits commands,
   telemetry, events and parameters; it does not emit state machines (checked
   against the generated `PolarisFswTopologyDictionary.json`, which contains no
   state-machine entries). The ground therefore has no data view of the machine
   at all, and there is no upstream visualization tool — `fpp-to-json` emits the
   AST, so a diagram generator would have to be written either way.

Against the four deciding criteria:

| criterion | FPP state machines | config → tables → executor |
|---|---|---|
| user-side visibility / editability | FPP is readable, but lives in the flight source tree and a change is a rebuild; guards are opaque C++ | one file in `config/`, guards are telemetry predicates, uplinkable as a table |
| SITL testability | a mode variant needs a rebuilt deployment | a variant is a different table; the same binary flies every row of the matrix |
| generated code vs tables | generated code, statically checked, fast — but the guard logic is outside it | interpreted tables, one interpreter to verify once, all logic inside the artifact |
| fault matrix table-verifiable | no: guards are not enumerable | yes: "which transitions can fire on this event" is a table query, and the SITL rows become a coverage check against it |

**Recommendation: the declarative-config path**, for the satellite-wide modes and
the GNC submodes. The deciding argument is the last row. §9's whole claim is that
every alert maps to a documented action; today that mapping is prose in this
document checked by hand against handlers, and the §23.1.1 fault matrix samples
it one injected fault at a time. With the machine as a table, the mapping is a
join — every FDIR event against the transitions it can trigger — and the fault
matrix's job changes from "demonstrate the responses we thought of" to "cover the
table", which is a coverage number rather than a judgement call.

FPP state machines remain the right tool for a machine that is **internal to one
component and not operator-facing** (a driver's initialisation sequence, a
calibration window's lifecycle), and nothing here forbids using them there. If
the executor's verification cost ever turns out to exceed the value of tabled
guards — the risk this decision carries — the fallback is FPP with the guard
vocabulary pushed into a shared, tested predicate library, which recovers most of
row 4 at the cost of rows 1 and 2.

#### 10.1.4 Visualization: the diagram is generated, never drawn

The config compiler emits a **state diagram from the same file it compiles the
tables from** — Mermaid (and DOT for the cases Mermaid renders badly) — which the
Sphinx build renders into the docs site next to the requirements it implements.
Consequences, which are the point rather than a side effect:

- the architecture is **reviewed as a diagram** and edited as one file, so a
  design discussion and a code review look at the same object;
- a diff to the machine is a diff to one declarative file, and the rendered
  diagram in the PR shows what it did;
- the diagram **cannot go stale**, because it is not a document — it is a build
  product of the artifact that flies. A hand-drawn state chart in a design
  document is wrong from the first push that touches the machine, and this is the
  standing complaint against every mode-manager diagram ever committed.

The same emitter serves the traceability matrix: each transition carries the
`REQ-` id(s) it implements, so the diagram can be rendered with unverified
transitions highlighted — the state-machine equivalent of the coverage gate
§22.2 already applies to requirements.

#### 10.1.5 Migration seam

Nothing is rewritten to get here. The hand-coded mode logic inside
`flight/PolarisFsw/AttitudeEstimator` — the coarse/fine arbitration, the §8.2
source ladder, the per-unit exclusion latches — **stays as it is**. It is the
first *instance* the target architecture describes, and the seam is already in
the right place: the estimator's mode transitions are reported as F´ events
(`FineModeEngaged`, `FineSourceChanged`, `FineModeDemoted`, the per-unit
exclusion edges), and its inputs are telemetry channels. Those events are exactly
the trigger kind the executor consumes and those channels exactly the guard
vocabulary, so lifting the estimator's ladder into a table later is a translation
with a behaviour-equivalence test, not a redesign. Until then the executor owns
the satellite-wide modes and the estimator owns its own submode — which is also
the safer order, since the estimator's ladder is the §10 Safe-mode floor and is
the last thing that should depend on new infrastructure.

---

## 11. Orbital Mechanics & Multi-Object Handling

### 11.1 Mission Planning
- **SGP4/TLE** propagation; **CCSDS OEM/OMM** ephemeris generation/ingest.
- **Ground-station contact prediction** (elevation mask, horizon geometry) — feeds link analysis (§16).
- **Eclipse prediction** windows — feeds power (§14).

### 11.2 Multi-Object Propagation & Tracking
- Onboard **secondary object catalog** of up to **N (~5)** objects, each from an uploaded **state vector + covariance** or **TLE**.
- Propagated with the onboard force model (or SGP4 for TLE-sourced), **with covariance propagation**.
- **Hooks (architectural now, empty):** relative geometry (range, range-rate, RIC offsets) and conjunction assessment (miss distance, Pc) — supports the future RPOD path.

### 11.3 Onboard Ephemeris & Earth Orientation
- Sun/Moon/planet positions onboard via **Chebyshev polynomial fits** generated on the ground from SPICE (DE440) and uploaded as coefficient sets per interval (§18.6).
- Onboard **EOP table** (UT1-UTC, polar motion) and **leap-second table** uploaded as low-rate parameters for ECI↔ECEF and UTC derivation.

**Implemented (Push 37), flight side.** The `OnboardTables` F´ component (`flight/PolarisFsw/OnboardTables/`) is the onboard table provider the Phase-4 GNC stack will read. At topology setup it loads all three tables from disk — leap seconds from the committed in-code IERS record (`time::LeapSecondTable::historical()`; no uploaded leap file exists yet), the IERS EOP table from the verbatim `finals.all` product windowed to the ephemeris span, and the Sun/Moon Chebyshev fits from the committed `de440_bodies.cheb` fixture (planets skipped) — and serves point queries over three typed ports: `getEopAt` (UT1-TAI + polar motion), `getBodyPosition` (Sun/Moon geocentric ECI metres, TAI→TDB inside), and `getTaiUtcOffset` (ΔAT). The load/validate/query logic lives in the flight-safe `lib/onboard` (`TableStore`), which only *wraps* the existing `lib/` tables and evaluators through their `addEntry`/`addSegment` contracts — no parsing or ephemeris math is re-implemented; `<cstdio>` file reads happen only at load/reload, never in steady state, and a malformed file is a rejected load with a warning EVR, never a throw or assert. Uploadability and persistence ride the filesystem: F´ FileUplink writes a new table file from the GDS, then the **`RELOAD_TABLES`** command re-runs load+validate. Reload is safe by construction — `TableStore` double-buffers two `TableSet` slots behind an atomic active index, staging a parse into the inactive slot and flipping only on full success, so a failed upload leaves the previous tables in service and a query never sees a half-loaded table. A `Svc.Sched` port on the housekeeping rate group runs a coverage-expiry check (a throttled `CoverageExpiring` warning when the current time runs past the EOP or ephemeris span). Health telemetry carries per-table entry/segment counts, coverage spans (TAI seconds), and load state. `OnboardTables` is a flight component (ships to hardware) — it lives outside the `PolarisSitl` subtopology and has no `lib/sitl` dependency. Exercised by `tests/unit/onboard_tables_test.cpp` (load/reload-swap/coverage/port answers vs the lib evaluators on the committed fixtures), the GNC consumer stand-in until Phase 4.

**Coarse fallbacks & source-quality grading (Implemented Push 38).** Table loss now degrades to **coarse operation instead of "no answer."** Every table query returns a **source-quality grade** — `kPrecise` (uploaded table), `kCoarse` (table-independent fallback), or `kUnavailable` (reserved) — surfaced through the F´ query ports (a `grade` member on `EopSample`/`PosEciMeters`) and per-domain telemetry. When the precise table cannot answer (not loaded, or the epoch is outside coverage), the store serves a fallback and reports `kCoarse` rather than failing: the Sun and Moon fall back to **low-precision analytic ephemerides** (Vallado §5.1 Algorithm 29 / §5.3.2, `lib/ephemeris/analytic_{sun,moon}` — mean-element polynomials that are pure functions of the clock, no data dependency, ~0.4° against the precise fit dominated by a deliberate mean-of-date≈J2000 frame approximation), and EOP falls back to a **zero-EOP** value (UT1 ≈ UTC, i.e. `UT1−TAI = −ΔAT`, and zero polar motion; bounded error |ΔUT1| ≤ 0.9 s and |polar motion| ≤ ~0.4 arcsec ≈ ~12 m ground projection). ΔAT itself **stays precise always** — it is an in-code IERS leap record held independently of the uploaded tables, so it (and the zero-EOP fallback that needs it) answer even before any load. This is the mechanism that makes the §10 Safe-mode floor — coarse sun pointing (SS + MAG + IMU, §8.1) — **star-tracker- and table-independent**: the coarse estimator can always obtain an inertial Sun reference from arithmetic alone. `OnboardTables` tracks the served grade per domain (ephemeris, EOP) on the housekeeping rate group and emits a throttled **`TableDegraded`** warning on a precise→coarse transition and a **`TableRecovered`** activity event on coarse→precise (e.g. after a successful `RELOAD_TABLES` restores coverage), with the current per-domain grade telemetered; the coverage-expiry watchdog now also reports the grade actually being served. Validated in `tests/unit/analytic_ephemeris_test.cpp` (analytic vs DE440 within 0.6°/1.0° for Sun/Moon across the fixture coverage) and the extended `onboard_tables_test.cpp` (coarse fallback values, grade on load/uncovered/unloaded, and always-precise ΔAT).

---

## 12. Mission Analysis Tools (Python, via pybind11)

Per §18.7, analysis tools **reuse the same C++/FSW code through pybind11 bindings** — Python exercises the exact flight implementations, minimizing reimplementation and divergence. These same bindings later power the live web tools (§21.4).

| Tool | Function |
|---|---|
| **RW/CMG momentum budgeting & sizing** *(implemented, Push 60)* | actuator envelope geometry (the exact zonotope, its inscribed radius — the momentum guaranteed in *every* direction — and the L2 ellipsoid inscribed in it), design validation against the sizing drivers with 30 % margin, the §5.3 analytic disturbance-torque budget split secular/cyclic, magnetorquer desaturation and detumble authority including the **B-dot measurement noise floor**, and the flight tuning the design implies, each with its formula and reasoning (`analysis/sizing/`) |
| **Detumble-time analysis** | time-to-detumble across initial rates, Monte Carlo dispersed |
| **Ground-station contact scheduling** | contact windows + schedule across the GS network |
| **Link budget / RF margin** | EIRP, path loss, pointing loss, RSSI, Eb/N0, margin per pass (§16) |
| **Pointing-budget / coverage** | end-to-end pointing error budget; optional ground-track coverage |
| **Post-processing & plotting** | standardized analysis of sim/MC data products |
| **Linear control analysis** *(implemented, Push 55)* | classical + disk stability margins of the as-flown sampled-data pointing loop, actuator-suite controllability (incl. wheel-failure subsets and the rods' instantaneous rank deficiency), and estimator observability — all read from the same committed `config/` YAML the FSW is tuned from (§8.5; `analysis/control/`) |

**The one exception to the pybind11 rule, stated so it does not become a habit.** `analysis/control/` and `analysis/sizing/` operate on *matrices and scalars read from config* — transfer functions, Gramians, rank tests, frequency responses; actuator envelope geometry, closed-form disturbance magnitudes, gain placement. They compute no quaternion, no frame transform and no propagation, so there is no flight implementation for them to drift from; what they analyse is the flight *design*, and they read that design from the same file the vehicle does rather than from a transcription. Where the plant already owns a datum, they read the plant's own file — `analysis/sizing` parses the exponential-atmosphere band table straight out of `sim/world/atmosphere.cpp`, and the wheel capacity and magnetometer noise out of `config/hardware/`. The moment such a tool needs a rotation or a trajectory it needs `bindings/`, not a NumPy copy.

**And a note on what `analysis/sizing/` found the first time it was run, because it is the argument for having built it.** On the reference vehicle *as it stood* it failed four criteria, none of which any test had been written on: the 5 deg/s tip-off needed 10.5 mN·m·s of wheel momentum against a 2.0 mN·m·s certified envelope; the RW-X wheels were ~78× their largest driver; the B-dot noise floor `σ√2/(Δt·|B|)` is **1.71 deg/s** at the orbit's weakest field against a committed `DetumbleExitRadps` of 0.50 deg/s, i.e. the detumble completion predicate sat below the rate at which B-dot's own input is noise; and — the finding that mattered most — that floor and the certified envelope were **mutually incompatible**, since the body momentum at the lowest certifiable rate already exceeded what the wheels were permitted to hold. No detumble exit threshold satisfied both bounds, which is a design conclusion rather than a tuning one. All four traced to one mis-sized part, and the vehicle took the re-sized 30 mN·m·s wheel and a 0.8 rad/s loop in the same push (§8.5, §19.2, §23.2); the tool now reports **PASS with zero failing criteria** on the committed configuration, and the two once-incompatible detumble bounds agree within 1 %.

---

## 13. Monte Carlo Framework

- Dispersions over initial conditions, mass properties, sensor/actuator errors, environment (space weather), and timing.
- Seeded, reproducible (§3.5); parallel execution; per-run config capture.
- **Pass/fail criteria with margin** (pointing-error percentiles, settling time, momentum/power margins) — margin against requirement thresholds is reported, not just pass/fail (§22.2).
- **Estimator consistency** metrics (NEES/NIS) aggregated across runs.
- Runnable as a CI smoke subset and as full off-line campaigns.

**The campaign pattern, as first built for detumble (Push 59).** A campaign whose runs are *closed-loop* — the truth sim lockstepped with the forked deployment — splits in two, and the split is a standing convention rather than one push's choice. **The runs are C++** (`tests/mc/`, a standalone driver per campaign) because the flight behaviour under study reaches the control law through the whole onboard chain: for B-dot that is the voted, plausibility-gated, quiet-window field the estimator publishes under the §7 duty-cycle interlock, and any sim-side reconstruction of that chain is a transcription of the flight path that drifts toward passing. **The statistics, figures and report are Python** (`analysis/<campaign>/`), which is what §13's dispersion/margin/consistency reporting means in practice, and needs no `bindings/` precisely because no GNC math crosses the line — only a JSONL record per run, written into `build-artifacts/` and never committed. Each record carries its own dispersion draw, its derived seed and the compiled `config_hash`, so it is re-flyable on its own (§3.5).
